Replication of Norovirus in Cell Culture Reveals a tropism for Dendritic Cells and Macrophages (Michael Emermean, Ed). PLoS Biology, 2, e^432. [SOURCE: Page_146]

5 [SOURCE: Page_146]

The experiment was carried out in a CMF greenhouse built in 1995 that was generally used for the cultivation of plants as food for aphids in an INRA insectarium. The greenhouse was equipped with a gas boiler for thermosiphon heating, ridge aeration, cooling boxes, and thermal screen for shading. Its climate was driven by an ARIA® system; the air temperature was recorded by a SIRIUS system. [SOURCE: Page_147]

Eighteen polypropylene planters 89.5  33  30 cm filled with a calcaric phaeozem were arranged along 3 lines of 6 planters, each line corresponding to one type of irrigation: (i) aerial irrigation from drippers about 40 cm from the soil surface, (ii) surface irrigation from at about 2 cm from the soil surface, and (iii) buried irrigation at about 4 cm below the soil surface (Figure IV.1 and Photo IV.1). 9 young plants were transplanted in each planter along a central line 8 cm apart between successive plants. They were initially abundantly watered to avoid plant death. From the fifth day before the contaminating irrigation, they were irrigated once a day in the morning during 2.5 minutes using aerial, surface or buried drip irrigation by using NDJ Click-Tif PC drippers with 1.1 L.hour-1 at about 0.1 MPa water overpressure (corresponding to 46 mL supplied to each plant or 2mm of irrigation water). Water was supplied from a tank (300 L capacity) through a CME 3-4 IKCCD AVBE-97755485 booster (Grundfos, Denmark) and polypropylene tubes. A common return to the 3 lines allowed bringing back water in the tank. [SOURCE: Page_147]

Figures IV.1: Experimental setup used for the monitoring of virus immobilisation. [SOURCE: Page_147]

Line inlet and outlet pressure gauges as well as inlet and outlet valves enabled to ensure approximately 0.01 MPa water overpressure. All irrigations were performed using virus-free groundwater, except for May 10, 2016 where the water was artificially contaminated. On this day, the pump was first disconnected from the main tank and the circuits were purged at low pressure so as to prevent dripping at the level of drippers. The pump was then connected to a 20 L secondary tank filled with about 10 L of the viral suspension, and the irrigation pipes were saturated at low pressure with this suspension. Following the contaminant irrigation, the [SOURCE: Page_147]

6 [SOURCE: Page_147]

irrigation pipes and the booster were rinsed at low water pressure by virus-free groundwater for about 5 min without any return of water to the main reservoir. [SOURCE: Page_148]

Photo IV.1: Experimental setup used for the monitoring of virus immobilisation. [SOURCE: Page_148]

Just before the contaminating irrigation, a wettable sewing thread was fixed to each aerial dripper and left in place until the end of experiment. It wrapped around the plant to ensure that irrigations (contaminating and non-contaminating later) directly reach the plant. [SOURCE: Page_148]

Virus distribution was monitored at six dates: the day before the contamination, approximately 2 h after contamination, and 1, 3, 7 and 10 days later. At each sampling date and for each type of irrigation, 1 planter with its 9 onions was used and condemned. In each planter and for each replicate, the upper and lower halves of the stem-leaves as well as the small young bulbs of onion plants were sampled separately; each of these plant portions was divided into 2 aliquots to elute viruses retained on the plant external surface and extract viruses internalized in the plant tissues, respectively. On the same planters, soil was sampled in the 0-4 cm and 4-20 cm horizons to monitor at the same dates the quantity of viral RNA and soil moisture. In addition, the day after the contamination, a map of soil moisture and viral RNA quantities was performed on a vertical slice of soil for each type of irrigation. [SOURCE: Page_148]

7 [SOURCE: Page_148]

To better understand our results, we monitored simultaneously (i) the virus concentration in the irrigation water, (ii) the soil moisture and temperature, and (iii) at the air temperature and relative moisture within the greenhouse, as well as the global radiation near the planters. [SOURCE: Page_149]

IV.2.c. Elution/extraction of viruses or viral RNA from plants and soil, concentration and purification [SOURCE: Page_149]

The elution and subsequent concentration of the viruses immobilized on the external surfaces of the green onions were performed according to a protocol almost identical to the protocol ISO/TS15216-133. In practice, 7-12 g of the upper part or the lower part of stem- leaves cut into 3-5 cm long pieces or 4-5 intact bulbs were put in a 400 mL Stomacher® filter bag with 40 mL of TGBE (Tris/Glycine/Beef Extract) buffer (100 mM Tris, 50 mM glycine, 1% beef extract) at pH=9.5 and submitted to continuous sway at room temperature for 20 minutes using a SSL$_3$ three-dimensional rocker (Stuart-equipment, Cole-Parmer, Staffordshire, UK) set at 60 rpm. The eluate was collected in a 50 mL conical tube and centrifuged at 10 000 g for 30 minutes at 4°C to clarify the suspension. The supernatant was then transferred to a new conical tube and its pH was adjusted to 7.0 by addition of 1 N HCl. Then, a volume of a solution of PEG-8000 (500 g.L-1) and NaCl (87.8 g.L-1) was added to the eluate in a ratio 1:4 (ie about 10 mL) to obtain a final concentration of 100 g.L-1 of PEG-8000 and 0.3 mol.L-1 of NaCl. The samples were then shaken for 60 s using a Vortex at maximum speed and the mixture was stirred during 1 hour using a Reax 2 rotary stirrer (Heidolph- Instruments GmbH, Schwabach, Germany) at a minimum speed. The viral particles, flocculated with PEG 8000, were then sedimented by centrifugation at 10 000 g for 30 minutes at 4°C. After removal of the supernatant, the virus-containing pellet was re- compacted and centrifuged at 10 000 g for 5 minutes at 4°C. The remaining pellet was resuspended in 500 μL of PBS at pH=7.334 and separated into three 140 μL aliquots to be stored at -86°C until use. [SOURCE: Page_149]

The extraction of viruses internalized in plants was adapted from the recent work of Di Caprio et al.35. After harvest, the viruses immobilized at the surface were destroyed by immersing and stirring the cut plant tissues successively in 30 mL of a solution of sodium hypochlorite (2000 ppm total chlorine) for 5 minutes at room temperature, then rinsing in 30 mL of ultrapure water for 5 minutes, and finally treated with 30 ml of 0.25 M sodium thiosulfate to neutralize the residual chlorine. Total chlorine levels were checked on certain dates. The samples were then milled after freezing in liquid nitrogen with an A11 analytical mill (IKA®-Werke GmbH & Co, Staufen, Germany). The obtained ground material was 33 ISO. 2012. Microbiology of food and animal feed - Horizontal method for determination of hepatitis A virus and norovirus in food using real-time RT-PCR - Part 1: Method for quantification. (ISO/TS 15216-1); 34 Deboosere N., Pinon A., Caudrelier Y., Delobel A., Merle G., Perelle S.? Belliot G. 2012. Adhesion of human pathogenic enteric viruses and surrogate viruses to inert and vegetal food surfaces. Food microbiology 32, 48-56. 35 DiCaprio E., Culbertson D., Li J. 2015. Evidence of the internalization of animal caliciviruses via the root of growing strawberry plants and dissemination to the fruit. Applied and Environmental Microbiology, AEM- 03867. [SOURCE: Page_149]

8 [SOURCE: Page_149]

suspended in 50 mL tubes in 5 mL of PBS at pH=7.0 and stirred for 2 minutes on a vortex. It was then centrifuged at 10 000 for 30 minutes to remove cell debris; the supernatant was transferred into 15 mL conical tubes and centrifuged one time more at 10 000 g for 30 minutes to remove remaining cell debris. The eluate was then aliquoted into three 140 μL samples which were frozen in liquid nitrogen and stored at -80°C. [SOURCE: Page_150]

We directly extracted the viral RNA from the soil rather than the whole viruses. The procedure of extraction, concentration and purification was derived from the method proposed by Griffiths et al^36. As far as possible, we took RNAse-free products and glassware to limit the risk of altering the viral RNA before its quantification. The extractions were carried out on 0.5 g samples of moist soil by addition of 0.5 mL of hexadecyltrimethylammonium bromide (CTAB) as extraction buffer and 0.5 mL of a phenol-chloroform-isoamyl alcohol mixture. (25:24:1) (pH 8.0). The CTAB extraction buffer was prepared by mixing equal volumes of 10% (w/v) CTAB (Sigma, Poole, UK) and pH 8 phosphate buffered saline (PBS) buffer (with 0.7 M NaCl and 240 mM potassium phosphate buffer, pH 8.0). The samples were then lysed by stirring on 100 MiniG® automated tissue homogenizer and cell lyser (SpexSamplePrep, Metuchen, NJ, USA) for 3 cycles of 30 s. The aqueous phase containing the RNA was separated by centrifugation (16 000 g) for 5 minutes at 4°C; it was then sampled and the phenol was removed by mixing with an equal volume of chloroform-isoamyl alcohol (24:1) followed by a new centrifugation (16 000 g) for 5 minutes at 4°C. All the RNA were then floculated by adding to the aqueous solution 2 volumes at 30% (w/v) of polyethylene glycol 6000 (Fluka BioChemika) and 1.6 M NaCl. The mixture was incubated with constant stirring using a stirrer Reax 2 rotary at minimum speed for 2 hours at laboratory temperature; it was then centrifuged (18 000 g) at 4°C for 10 minutes. The pellet of flocculated nucleic acids were then washed in 70% (v/v) ethanol cooled on ice, and air-dried before resuspending in 50 μL of RNase-free Tris-EDTA buffer (pH 7,4) (Severn Biotech, Kidderminster, United Kingdom). [SOURCE: Page_150]

IV.3. Results and discussions [SOURCE: Page_150]

IV.3.a. Greenhouse climate, water supplied during irrigation, water contamination [SOURCE: Page_150]

Temperatures fluctuated between 13 and 34°C from the day before the contaminating irrigation to the last sampling day. Likewise, the air relative moisture fluctuated between 20 and more than 90% (Figures IV.2). It has been clearly demonstrated that relative air moistures between about 20 and 75% can cause the quick inactivation of some viruses^12, and that high temperatures can accelerate the phenomenon. Radiation fluctuated but probably did not directly participate to the inactivation of viruses, as virus RNA can be altered by UV radiation, especially C-type UV, which are stopped by greenhouse canopies. [SOURCE: Page_150]

11 [SOURCE: Page_152]

An experimental evaluation of the quantities of water supplied was carried out on the day of the irrigation and the following days for the 9 drippers used for the aerial irrigation of the green onions sampled the day before the contaminating irrigation. The results presented in Figure IV.3 clearly indicate that the irrigation doses were similar from one day to another and close to the calculated value (45.8 mL) for the nominal flow of the drippers. [SOURCE: Page_153]

Since murine norovirus is naturally present in the environment, we characterized its concentration in the irrigation water the day before the contamination, the day of the contaminating irrigation (at the level of the contaminated water supply tank and the level of 9 drippers) and the following days (Figure IV.4). Murine noroviruses were naturally present in the drilling water used for irrigation (before its artificial contamination), but well below the concentrations used for the calibration range of RT-qPCR (Figure IV.4). Virus concentration in the contaminated tank was identical to that of water coming out of the drippers the day of the contaminating irrigation; similarly, the concentration of murine noroviruses in the dripping water was only one-thousandth of the contaminating concentration the day after the contaminant irrigation. [SOURCE: Page_153]

Our experimental protocol thus enabled to ensure the homogeneity of virus concentrations along the irrigation lines before the beginning of the contaminating irrigation, all the more the concentrations were tested near the end of the line (5th planter in the line devoted to aerial irrigation).And the same procedure enabled to ensure almost perfect rinsing of the irrigation pipes just after the contaminant irrigation. The abatement of 3 log^10 suggests a dilution per 1000 of a residue of contaminated water which then remained in the more or less closed circuit. However, we notice some incidents: (1) a significant water leak near one of the buried drippers which certainly increased the soil moisture of this planter and the quantity of viruses in the soil of this planter, and (2) a leak at the level of an aerial dripper that appeared a few days before the end of the experiment. Thus, about 5.8 107 viruses were supplied per onion (for 45.8 mL of water having about 106.1 GC.mL-1) were provided. [SOURCE: Page_153]

IV.3.b. External and internal onion contaminations [SOURCE: Page_153]

Viral contaminations of the external surfaces of green onions have been reported on Figures IV.5. Aerial and surface irrigations have resulted in very similar or even identical contaminations of the bulbs (Figure IV.5a). The level of contamination reached about 106-107 GC.g-1 of fresh onion. As the bulbs were still young and barely trained, these concentrations corresponded to about 4.7 106 GC.bulb-1, i.e. 8-9% of the quantity of viruses supplied with irrigation. By contrast, underground irrigation induced only a few contamination of the bulbs (about 10 3- 104 GC.g-1 of fresh onion) as the drippers were under the bulbs. Moreover, the absence of surface contamination of the bulbs sampled 7 and 10 days after the contamination probably reflects more the random nature of the contamination when it results from underground [SOURCE: Page_153]

12 [SOURCE: Page_153]

irrigation. Beyond the initial contamination of the bulbs for the aerial and surface irrigations, one then observes a decrease of contamination almost identical for these 2 modes of irrigation that follows an exponential decay, suggesting a disappearance of virus proportional to the amount of remaining viruses and caused by virus-free irrigations after the contaminating day. In our experimental conditions, virus removal was about 0.3 log^10 per day. Viral contaminations of the external surfaces of the lower halves of the stem-leaves could a priori be neglected for surface and buried irrigations (Figure IV.5b), the non-zero values resulting surely from inadvertent contamination either during onion sampling or more probably later during laboratory steps of this work. Moreover, their contaminations (less than or equal to 102 GC.g-1) are extremely low (values well below the minimum concentration used to calibrate the RT-qPCR). By contrast, aerial irrigation strongly contaminated the lower halves of the stem-leaves (about 104-105 GC.g-1), although contamination levels remained lower than those obtained on the bulbs. The peak concentrations achieved amounted to approximately 7.0 104 viruses per plant, corresponding approximately to 0.1% of the quantity of viruses initially brought to each plant. The weakness of the levels reached can be explained by the fast transit of the drops of water along the stem-leaves (contrary to the case of bulbs that could be surrounded by stagnant water). In the days following the contaminating irrigation, the decrease of contamination could be described by an exponential decay, with a removal of about 1 log^10 per day that was higher than that observed for the bulbs. It could probably be related to the rinsing of the stems by the irrigated irrigation water. However, another track should be mentioned: the greenhouse was equipped with a powerful fan that participate to the temperature regulation; this fan may have caused the partial aerosolization of viruses (even if this fan did not blow directly on our "table" and the aerial irrigation line was at the "table" edge opposite to the fan and close to the glass walls of the greenhouse that can slow down the wind). Viral contamination of the external surfaces of the upper halves of the stem-leaves was observed for aerial irrigation with a maximum of about 105-106 GC.g-1 (Figure IV.5c); this level was similar or slightly higher than that for the lower stem-leaves. The decrease rate of the contamination was initially similar to that for the halves lower stem-leaves during the first 3 days following contamination. Beyond this date, contamination seemed to stabilize. Punctual contaminations for surface and buried irrigations surely resulted from involuntary contaminations in the greenhouse or the laboratory. It should be noted that onion stem-leaves sometimes bent and could touch the soil surface; the phenomenon was probably more pronounced for the so-called surface irrigation, for which the stem-leaves sometimes had to make their way around the dripper pipe placed a few cm above the surface of the soil. [SOURCE: Page_154]

Viral contaminations within green onion tissues after internalizations have been reported on Figures IV.6a-c. [SOURCE: Page_154]

0 ­2   0   2   4     6       8           10 DAY Figures IV.6a-c: Evolution of the internalized of (a) the bulbs, (b) the lower halves of the stem-leaves, and (c) the upper halves of the stem-leaves. (The purple vertical bar corresponds to the date of contamination). [SOURCE: Page_156]

15 [SOURCE: Page_156]

Concerning the bulbs (Figure IV.6a), virus internalization seemed to be similar for the three types of irrigation during the first 3 days after the contaminant irrigation. In particular, there is a priori no difference between internalization for buried irrigation and internalization for drippers above the soil surface. The lack of difference probably results from a good colonization of the soil by the roots (results not shown) allowing the green onion plants to absorb water (and viruses) coming from the drippers whatever their position. The maximum levels of contamination were approximately 10 times lower than the levels of external surface contaminations (when expressed per g of fresh product). On the 3rd day after the contaminating irrigation, we could no longer detect viruses internalized within bulbs; their disappearance suggests their migration by convection with the flow of raw sap. The concentrations observed 7 and 10 days after the contaminating irrigation could have resulted from sample contamination at any stage of the experiment between plant sampling and final RT-qPCR analysis. Concerning the lower halves of the stem-leaves (Figure IV.6b) and ignoring an outlier value for surface irrigation the day before the contamination, we observed as a first approximation a similar internalizations of viruses for the three types of irrigations with a maximum reached on the day of the contamination (surface irrigation and aerial irrigation) or the following day (buried irrigation) reaching 103-104 GC.g-1. This contamination seems more durable over time than the contamination of the bulbs with a slow decrease in concentrations with time (virus removal between 0.2 and 0.9 log^10 per day). Concerning the upper halves of the stem-leaves (Figure IV.6c), we observed similar internalizations for the three types of irrigations up to 3 days after the contamination. The maximum concentrations reached the day of the contamination and/or the next day were approximately 103-104 GC.g-1, i.e. values similar to those obtained for the lower halves of the stem-leaves. Beyond the third day after infection, the measurements seemed to diverge with zero values suggesting a rapid disappearance of the viruses, but also values slightly lower than those of the day of the contamination or the next day. Thus, it remains difficult to conclude whether the internalized viruses disappeared rapidly or not from the lower parts of the plants by convection with the flow of raw sap and, if so, whether they can accumulate at the end of the metaxylem, or if they can to be expelled with transpiration or with guttation. Other questions included the possible effect of essential oils detected in green onions on [SOURCE: Page_157]

IV.4. Conclusions and perspectives [SOURCE: Page_157]

The objectives of this work were (i) to evaluate the impact of green onion irrigation on the initial distribution of viruses in the soil and plant, and (ii) to characterize the subsequent fate of the viruses introduced (transfers and inactivation). [SOURCE: Page_157]

While surface contamination greatly depends on the type of irrigation (i.e. aerial, surface and buried drip irrigation), internalized contamination was as a first approximation independent of the type of irrigation. After the initial contamination, viruses immobilized at [SOURCE: Page_157]

16 [SOURCE: Page_157]

the surface of the bulbs and stem-leaves could be progressively eluted with subsequent virus- free irrigation. Virus removal could be described by an exponential decay, suggesting that it was proportional to the quantity of viruses remaining immobilized and to the irrigation amount. The existence of viruses still internalized beyond the first 3 days after contamination was less certain, and we still have to work on their fate within plants, as wastewater management (standards), agricultural practices (minimum delay between the last contaminating irrigation and the harvest …) and post-harvest treatment of products (washing, removal of certain portions of plants) should be optimized to actual health risks. [SOURCE: Page_158]

Until now, we couldn't assess the proportions of viruses that were still infectious, for various reasons including probably the presence of essential oils that affect RAW cell cultures and maybe viruses themselves^37. However, it is probable that viruses immobilized on the surface of stem-leaves of green onions were inactivated as a consequence of the relative air moisture values; the inactivation of viruses at the surface of onion bulbs would depend on the relative moisture of the soil gas phase that may be saturated as long as soil aggregates remained saturated. It is unclear whether internalized viruses remained infectious or not, as another work^38 has already noted the internalization of murine noroviruses within the same green onions (Allium fistulosum) using plaque assay to quantify virus quantities in the plant tissues. [SOURCE: Page_158]

20   ABSTRACT. Contamination of conventional water bodies by human enteric viruses coming [SOURCE: Page_163]

21   from wastewater discharge is a well-established phenomenon. Here we propose a model of viral [SOURCE: Page_163]

22   contamination of rivers based on acute gastroenteritis epidemiology and assess whether it can [SOURCE: Page_163]

23   simulate in situ experimental monitoring. Noroviruses, rotaviruses, enteroviruses, adenoviruses [SOURCE: Page_163]

24   and hepatitis A viruses were quantified by molecular methods after water concentration. Water [SOURCE: Page_163]

25   flows were obtained from the Hydro databank and the wastewater treatment plant (WWTP) data. [SOURCE: Page_163]

26   Acute gastroenteritis cases based on medical prescriptions were provided by Santé Publique [SOURCE: Page_163]

27   France. We estimated the total number of daily viral acute gastroenteritis cases and modeled [SOURCE: Page_163]

28   virus shedding and fate in the WWTP and rivers. Simulated virus concentrations were compared [SOURCE: Page_163]

29   to the weighted sum of measured concentrations. Seasonal variations in viral acute gastroenteritis [SOURCE: Page_163]

30   were predicted from vomiting criteria. All viruses except hepatitis A virus were widely detected [SOURCE: Page_163]

31   in wastewaters and river, in concentrations reaching 10+8 GC.L-1 for adenoviruses in the Artière [SOURCE: Page_163]

32   River. We were able to predict virus load in raw wastewater and in the Artière River. Estimated [SOURCE: Page_163]

33   weighting coefficients showed the high impact of noroviruses GII. This model can thus serve to [SOURCE: Page_163]

34   compare water treatment, discharge and reuse scenarios. [SOURCE: Page_163]

35 [SOURCE: Page_163]

4 [SOURCE: Page_163]

## 36   1. INTRODUCTION

37   Conventional water resources are increasingly scarce and increasingly polluted, including from [SOURCE: Page_164]

38   contamination by human pathogenic enteric viruses^1–6. Viral contamination of urban rivers [SOURCE: Page_164]

39   results essentially from wastewater discharges 3 from sewerage systems and wastewater treatment [SOURCE: Page_164]

40   plants (WWTP)7. Improved sanitation, which had already reached 40% of the global population [SOURCE: Page_164]

41   in 20107, drives wastewater reclamation^8,9. Wastewater management has to secure sanitary [SOURCE: Page_164]

42   safety. Models combining the epidemiology of viral acute gastroenteritis (AGE), shedding and [SOURCE: Page_164]

43   fate in sewers, WWTP and rivers could help assess health risks of different wastewater [SOURCE: Page_164]

44   management scenarios. [SOURCE: Page_164]

45        To our knowledge, three models have sought to predict enteric virus contamination of rivers 10– 12 46    : Mohammed et al.11,12 proposed two similar black box models based on environmental [SOURCE: Page_164]

47   variables unrelated to AGE, whereas Petterson et al. 10 combined, for a Norwegian river, its [SOURCE: Page_164]

48   potential norovirus contamination estimated from fecal indicators with local prevalence data on [SOURCE: Page_164]

49   confirmed norovirus AGE cases in Sweden. This second approach was limited by variations in [SOURCE: Page_164]

50   fecal indicator organisms in treated wastewater and their environmental fate, and by possible [SOURCE: Page_164]

51   differences in norovirus prevalence between the diagnosed foreign subpopulation and the whole [SOURCE: Page_164]

52   sick population. As pathogens are rarely identified via stool specimens, estimating the number of [SOURCE: Page_164]

53   viral AGE cases requires symptoms statistically discriminating viral-etiology AGE from others [SOURCE: Page_164]

54   (the most discriminating symptoms being vomiting, fever and mucous diarrhea 13), then [SOURCE: Page_164]

55   estimating the proportions of these symptoms in the global sick population from a well-known [SOURCE: Page_164]

56   subpopulation (e.g. diagnosed or consulting a physician) and the impact of these symptoms on [SOURCE: Page_164]

57   the probability of being a membership of this subpopulation 14, and then the proportion of viral [SOURCE: Page_164]

58   AGE in the whole population. The proportions of asymptomatic carriers and the kinetics of virus [SOURCE: Page_164]

5 [SOURCE: Page_164]

59   shedding are not well known for each virus species, but quantitative results exist for human [SOURCE: Page_165]

60   noroviruses^15–21 that are the leading cause of AGE22–25, and Teunis et al.21 proposed a stochastic [SOURCE: Page_165]

61   model of virus shedding for symptomatic and asymptomatic norovirus GII.4 carriers. The [SOURCE: Page_165]

62   subsequent fate of viruses in sewers, WWTP and rivers spans transport, immobilization and [SOURCE: Page_165]

63   inactivation. In sewers, virus concentrations may be affected by leakages 26,27, dilution with [SOURCE: Page_165]

64   stormwaters, reducing conditions and iron oxides^28, and wastewater residence time that often [SOURCE: Page_165]

65   does not exceed a few hours^29,30. In rivers, virus removal may be neglected over a few hours, in [SOURCE: Page_165]

66   which time a virus can travel a few km^31. For combined sewer systems collecting stormwaters, [SOURCE: Page_165]

67   WWTP bypass discharges raw wastewater into the environment. Several meta-analyses 32,33 and [SOURCE: Page_165]

68   reviews^34,35 have dealt with virus removal in WWTPs, and one study has compared a model [SOURCE: Page_165]

69   against experimental data^36. Classical pre-treatments and activated sludge WWTP treatments [SOURCE: Page_165]

70   generally enable between < 1 log^1037 and 3-4 log^1034 reduction in human enteric viruses, but virus [SOURCE: Page_165]

71   removal is very virus-dependent^38. Tertiary physical treatments and/or chemical treatments [SOURCE: Page_165]

72   enable additional virus removal^35. [SOURCE: Page_165]

73     Our objectives here were to (i) propose a model of viral contamination of water combining [SOURCE: Page_165]

74   AGE epidemiology, virus shedding and virus fate in the ‘sewers–WWTP–surface water’ [SOURCE: Page_165]

75   continuum, and (ii) assess whether this model correctly predicts in situ contamination in a small [SOURCE: Page_165]

76   region of France. [SOURCE: Page_165]

77 [SOURCE: Page_165]

## 78   2. MATERIALS AND METHODS

79     Sampling zone and climate context. The work was carried out on a small geographical [SOURCE: Page_165]

80   area near the city of Clermont-Ferrand, France (45.77 N, 3.09 E) combining the collection basin [SOURCE: Page_165]

81   of wastewaters conveyed from 19 towns and cities (237,700 total inhabitants in 2014) to the [SOURCE: Page_165]

6 [SOURCE: Page_165]

82   'Trois Rivières' WWTP, the Artière River in which treated wastewaters are partly discharged but [SOURCE: Page_166]

83   receiving upstream treated wastewater from a small WWTP (120 population equivalents), and a [SOURCE: Page_166]

84   1500-ha area irrigated by the other part of treated wastewaters (representing 30–40% of WWTP [SOURCE: Page_166]

85   effluent flow rate during the irrigation period^39) after an additional lagooning (Figure S$_1$). The [SOURCE: Page_166]

86    'Trois Rivières' WWTP was designed for 425,000 population equivalents. Its treatment train [SOURCE: Page_166]

87    includes a pre-treatment (screening followed by sand and oil removals), a conventional very- [SOURCE: Page_166]

88   low-load biological activated sludge process with enhanced nitrification/denitrification, and a [SOURCE: Page_166]

89   clarification step during which iron chlorosulfate is used to precipitate phosphate. [SOURCE: Page_166]

90     Climate is semi-continental. From September 2015 to August 2016, cumulated rainfall was [SOURCE: Page_166]

91   564 mm, and mean temperature was 12.3°C at the Clermont-Ferrand airport climate station. [SOURCE: Page_166]

92   Additional data concerning the Artière River flow upstream of wastewater discharge (45.77 N, [SOURCE: Page_166]

93   3.14 E) was obtained from the Hydro databank run by the French Ministry for Ecology, Energy [SOURCE: Page_166]

94   and Sustainable Development^40. Data concerning wastewater flows at the inlet and outlet of the [SOURCE: Page_166]

95   WWTP were provided by the WWTP. We assumed that the Artière River flow downstream of [SOURCE: Page_166]

96   wastewater discharge was equal to the sum of the Artière River flow upstream of discharge, the [SOURCE: Page_166]

97   treated wastewater flow at the WWTP outlet minus water withdrawal for filling the lagoons, and [SOURCE: Page_166]

98   the WWTP by-pass flow. During the same period (September 2015 to August 2016), Artière [SOURCE: Page_166]

99   river flow upstream of the treated wastewater discharge varied between 4320 and [SOURCE: Page_166]

100   140,832 m^3.day-1 and was generally more than doubled by treated wastewater discharge (that [SOURCE: Page_166]

101   varied between 29,007 and 144,467 m^3.day-1). The mean downstream Artière flow–to–upstream [SOURCE: Page_166]

102   Artière flow ratio was equal to 5.13. During the same annual period, the WWTP by-pass was [SOURCE: Page_166]

103   operated for 60 days with a maximum discharge flow of 10,500 m^3.day-1. [SOURCE: Page_166]

7 [SOURCE: Page_166]

104     Experimental design and protocols. The experimental monitoring was carried out [SOURCE: Page_167]

105   between 1st June 2015 and 31 August 2016 once a month and once every two weeks outside the [SOURCE: Page_167]

106   period of irrigation by treated wastewater and during this period, respectively. We sampled raw [SOURCE: Page_167]

107   wastewater just after screening (i.e. before sand and oil removal), treated wastewater at the [SOURCE: Page_167]

108   WWTP outlet, and Artière River water upstream and downstream of the WWTP discharge [SOURCE: Page_167]

109   (Figure S$_1$). The temperature and the pH of these waters were measured in the same time. [SOURCE: Page_167]

110     Environmental concentrations of noroviruses (genogroup I, NVGI; genogroup II, NVGII), [SOURCE: Page_167]

111   rotaviruses A (RV-A), enteroviruses (EV), adenoviruses (AdV) and hepatitis A viruses (HAV) [SOURCE: Page_167]

112   were monitored using practically the same water sampling–virus concentration–purification [SOURCE: Page_167]

113   procedure as in Wurtzer et al.41 based on US Environmental Protection Agency method 161542,43. [SOURCE: Page_167]

114     Briefly, water samples were concentrated by three successive steps. River samples (100 L) [SOURCE: Page_167]

115   were filtered in situ using electropositive filters (NanoCeram® VS$_2$.5-5, Argonide Corporation, [SOURCE: Page_167]

116   Sanford, FL) at a rate of 2 L. minute-1, after which the filters were returned to the lab, and [SOURCE: Page_167]

117   wastewater samples (1 L) were filtered in-lab using disc filters (NanoCeram® 90MM NC Disk [SOURCE: Page_167]

118   NL, Argonide) at a rate ensuring approximately the same flux of water as for in situ filtration. [SOURCE: Page_167]

119   Viruses were then eluted by sonicating for 1 hour the filters immersed in 450 mL (river water [SOURCE: Page_167]

120   samples) or 50 mL (wastewater samples) of a beef extract elution buffer adjusted to pH=9.5 and [SOURCE: Page_167]

121   kept below 10°C. The buffer solutions with eluted viruses were then recovered and their pH was [SOURCE: Page_167]

122   adjusted to 3.5. They were then gently stirred during 1 hour at laboratory temperature before [SOURCE: Page_167]

123   being centrifuged. After centrifugation, the pellets were resuspended in 8 mL phosphate buffer [SOURCE: Page_167]

124   pH=9.0, and the resulting viral suspension was successively clarified by centrifugation and [SOURCE: Page_167]

125   filtration on low-binding 0.45 µm filters. The suspensions were concentrated one last time and [SOURCE: Page_167]

8 [SOURCE: Page_167]

126   their beef extract partly removed by ultracentrifugation at 143,000 g for 2 h at 4°C on a 40% [SOURCE: Page_168]

127   sucrose cushion. The final pellet was resuspended in the remaining 0.6 mL sucrose cushion. [SOURCE: Page_168]

128     Molecular detection and quantification of virus genome copies (GC) was performed according [SOURCE: Page_168]

129   to Prevost et al.3 but with an additional PCR inhibitor removal step using a One-Step PCR [SOURCE: Page_168]

130   inhibitor removal kit (ref. D6030, Zymo Research, Irvine, CA) following the manufacturer’s [SOURCE: Page_168]

131   instructions. Primers and probes employed for molecular detection were designed as previously [SOURCE: Page_168]

132   described^3, except for the NVGI probe which was corrected as follows: 5'-FAM- [SOURCE: Page_168]

133   CGA{T}CYCC{T}GTC{C}ACA-BHQ$_1$-3' (the base between brackets corresponds to LNA [SOURCE: Page_168]

134   base). Detection thresholds were about 102.1, 100.4, 102.2, 100.5, 102.6 and 101.6 GC.L-1 for NVGI, [SOURCE: Page_168]

135   NVGII, RV-A, EV, AdV and HAV, respectively. When 1 L of water was sampled, these [SOURCE: Page_168]

136   thresholds were a hundred times higher. [SOURCE: Page_168]

137     Additional water aliquots were sampled to quantify Escherichia coli (500 mL) at the Vaucluse [SOURCE: Page_168]

138   departmental analysis lab in Avignon (France), total organic C and N (180 mL), anions and [SOURCE: Page_168]

139   cations (200 mL), and NH$_4$+ (40 mL). [SOURCE: Page_168]

140     Epidemiological data and probabilistic estimation of viral AGE incidence. AGE [SOURCE: Page_168]

141   cases based on medical prescriptions were provided by Santé Publique France for the 19 cities [SOURCE: Page_168]

142   connected to the WWTP. For each declared AGE case, information includes age bracket (1–4, 5– [SOURCE: Page_168]

143   65 or >65 yrs), place of residence, and medical prescription (antiemetic, antidiarrheal, antiemetic [SOURCE: Page_168]

144   and antidiarrheal, oral rehydration solution (ORS) for children aged under 15 yrs, and ‘Others’, [SOURCE: Page_168]

145   i.e. antispasmodics and intestinal adsorbents). Note that antiemetics are contraindicated and [SOURCE: Page_168]

146   rarely prescribed for children in France^44,45. In 2014, the age-bracket stratification of inhabitants [SOURCE: Page_168]

147   was 12,100 aged 1–4 yrs, 184,600 aged 5–65 yrs and 41,000 aged >65 yrs. [SOURCE: Page_168]

9 [SOURCE: Page_168]

149   these inhabitants from the total number of new viral AGE cases n AGE(d) and the proportion of [SOURCE: Page_169]

150   new viral AGE cases pAGE(v,d): [SOURCE: Page_169]

177   where �AGE (vom. ) is mean probability of people with AGE experiencing vomiting. �AGE (vom. ) [SOURCE: Page_170]

178   was set at 0.62914. �AGE|prscr. (vom. ) and �AGE|prscr. (vom. ̅̅̅̅̅̅ ) were estimated from [SOURCE: Page_170]

179   epidemiological data between 1st September 2015 and 31st August 2016 (0.79 and 0.21, [SOURCE: Page_170]

180                                               ̅̅̅̅̅̅̅. (prscr. ) were estimated as 0.419 and 0.190, respectively). �AGE|vom. (prscr. ) and �AGE|vom [SOURCE: Page_170]

182     Viral shedding, transport and removal modeling. The daily quantity of viruses shed [SOURCE: Page_170]

183   from one symptomatic carrier Qp-sympt.(v,t) (GC.day-1.people-1) was estimated from virus [SOURCE: Page_170]

184   concentration C(t) (GC.g-1 stool) within stools and daily quantity of stool ms-sympt.(t) (g.day-1) [SOURCE: Page_170]

185   excreted: [SOURCE: Page_170]

186   �p−sympt (v, �) = �(�) × �s−sympt. (�)                        (6) [SOURCE: Page_170]

187   where t is time (day) after onset of infection. ms-sympt.(t) was set at 500 g.day-1 during the first 3 [SOURCE: Page_170]

188   days of AGE, then at 100 g.day-1 thereafter^10. Assuming that virus concentrations in stools did [SOURCE: Page_170]

189   not distinguish symptomatic from asymptomatic carriers 21 and ignoring the variability between [SOURCE: Page_170]

190   infected people, the daily quantity of viruses shed for a symptomatic carrier and the [SOURCE: Page_170]

191   corresponding proportion of asymptomatic carriers Qp(v,t) (GC.day-1.p-1) is then: [SOURCE: Page_170]

11 [SOURCE: Page_170]

�(asympt.) 192   �p (v, �) = �(�) × (�s−sympt. (�) + (ms−asympt. (�) × (                  )))         (7) 1−�(asympt.) [SOURCE: Page_171]

193   where ms-asympt(t) is daily quantity of stool for asymptomatic carriers (g.day-1), and p(asympt.) is [SOURCE: Page_171]

194   proportion of asymptomatic carriers among infected people. ms-asympt.(t) was set at 100 g.day-1 all [SOURCE: Page_171]

195   the time^10. Although proportion of asymptomatic carriers and virus shedding are virus- [SOURCE: Page_171]

196   dependent^46–48, we used the data for NVGII that explained most of the experimental trends 49: [SOURCE: Page_171]

197   p(asympt.) was set at 0.3021, and virus shedding of symptomatic and asymptomatic carriers was [SOURCE: Page_171]

198   described by the model of Teunis et al.21: [SOURCE: Page_171]

199   �(�) = �max × fac × � −α×� × (1 − � −(β−α)×� )                (8) [SOURCE: Page_171]

200   where Cmax is maximum virus concentration reached (GC.g-1 stool), and  (day-1),  (day-1) and [SOURCE: Page_171]

201   fac are three interlinked constants as proposed by Teunis et al.21. Cmax, ,  and fac values were [SOURCE: Page_171]

202   set at 10+7 GC.g-1 stool, 0.53 day-1, 0.48 day-1 and –27.2, respectively, in order to fit with the [SOURCE: Page_171]

203   median relationship proposed by Teunis et al.21. We neglected virus removal in the sewerage [SOURCE: Page_171]

204   system and the Artière River. Daily flow of viruses at the WWTP inlet, i.e. Q(v,t) (GC.day-1), [SOURCE: Page_171]

205   could then be estimated with the following equation: � 206   �(v, �) = ∑�=0 day �p (v, �) × �AGE (v, � + �prscr. − �sew − �)                   (9) [SOURCE: Page_171]

207   where nday is duration of virus shedding (40 day), dprscr. is delay from onset of shedding to [SOURCE: Page_171]

208   physician consultation (day), and dsew is sewer residence time (day). As average dprscr. was about [SOURCE: Page_171]

209   1.5 days^14 and dsew is often no longer than a few hours^29,30, we neglected the difference dprscr. – [SOURCE: Page_171]

210   dsew. [SOURCE: Page_171]

211     Virus removal in the WWTP was simulated in the pretreatment basins, the three aeration zones [SOURCE: Page_171]

212   of the activated sludge basins, and the clarifiers, assuming in each basin the mixture of incoming [SOURCE: Page_171]

213   water with water already present and a first-order kinetic varying with wastewater temperature [SOURCE: Page_171]

12 [SOURCE: Page_171]

214   according to a Q10 law (Supporting Information). The simulation of virus concentrations in the [SOURCE: Page_172]

215   Artière River downstream of the wastewater discharge took into account the mixing of treated [SOURCE: Page_172]

216   wastewater with Artière River water, the discharge of raw wastewater during by-pass periods, [SOURCE: Page_172]

217   and the withdrawal of treated wastewater to fill the lagoons used for crop irrigation. By doing [SOURCE: Page_172]

218   this, we neglected other contributions to Artière River flows, including small stream [SOURCE: Page_172]

219   contributions (mainly Le Bec stream), surface runoff during rainy periods, and exchanges [SOURCE: Page_172]

220   between rivers and alluvial aquifers. [SOURCE: Page_172]

221     Indirect comparison between simulated virus fate and experimental virus [SOURCE: Page_172]

222   concentrations. To enable comparisons between virus concentrations simulated without [SOURCE: Page_172]

223   distinguishing viral species and experimental data for various enteric viruses, we defined [SOURCE: Page_172]

224   equivalent experimental virus concentrations [veq,d] (GC.L-1) from the measured concentrations [SOURCE: Page_172]

225   at date d: [SOURCE: Page_172]

226   [Veq , �] = a × [NVGI, �] + b × [NoVGII, �] + c × [RV­A, �] + d × [EV, �] + e × [AdV, �] 227            (10) [SOURCE: Page_172]

228   where [NVGI,d], [NVGII,d], [RV-A,d], [EV,d] and [AdV,d] are the concentrations (GC.L-1) of [SOURCE: Page_172]

229   NVGI, NVGII, RV, EV and AdV, respectively, and a, b, c, d and e are five weighting [SOURCE: Page_172]

230   coefficients that express their relative impact on AGE. These coefficients were estimated by [SOURCE: Page_172]

231   fitting equivalent virus concentrations to simulated virus concentrations in raw wastewater, by [SOURCE: Page_172]

232   minimizing the sum of square differences between viral concentrations simulated from [SOURCE: Page_172]

233   epidemiological data and equivalent virus concentrations derived from equation (10). The same [SOURCE: Page_172]

234   coefficients were used thereafter to compare measured virus concentrations against simulated [SOURCE: Page_172]

235   virus concentrations in the Artière River. [SOURCE: Page_172]

236 [SOURCE: Page_172]

## 237   3. RESULTS AND DISCUSSION

13 [SOURCE: Page_172]

238     Number of viral AGE cases estimated from epidemiological data. Approximately [SOURCE: Page_173]

239   one tenth of the 237,700 inhabitants of the wastewater collection basin consulted a physician due [SOURCE: Page_173]

240   to AGE over the period 1st September 2015 – 31st August 2016; this proportion decreased with [SOURCE: Page_173]

241   increasing age-bracket (Table 1). [SOURCE: Page_173]

242   Table 1. Statistical data on AGE leading to physician consultation and medical prescription as a [SOURCE: Page_173]

243   function of age-group over the September 1st 2015–August 31st 2016 period. [SOURCE: Page_173]

Age group (years)                  <5      5–65    > 65   All ages [SOURCE: Page_173]

p(AGE with prescription)               0.38    0.09    0.04     0.10 [SOURCE: Page_173]

pAGEprscr.(antiemetic only)          0.29    0.43    0.36     0.39 [SOURCE: Page_173]

pAGE prscr.(antidiarrheal only)        0.24    0.17    0.26     0.19 [SOURCE: Page_173]

pAGEconsult.(antiemetic and antidiarrheal)   0.20    0.33    0.25     0.30 [SOURCE: Page_173]

pAGE prscr.(ORS <1 year)            0.02     0       0       0.004 [SOURCE: Page_173]

pAGE prscr.(ORS 1-15 years)           0.24    0.002    0       0.05 [SOURCE: Page_173]

pAGE prscr.(‘Others’)             0.02    0.07    0.13     0.07 [SOURCE: Page_173]

pAGE prscr.(vomiting symptom)*          0.67    0.82    0.70     0.79 [SOURCE: Page_173]

pAGE(vomiting symptom)**                0.48    0.67    0.51     0.63 [SOURCE: Page_173]

pAGE(viral infection)***              0.00    1.00    0.00     0.77 244   *assessed from the sum of pAGE(antiemetic) + pAGE(antiemetic and antidiarrheal) and ignoring the [SOURCE: Page_173]

245   ORS and ‘Others’ groups; **using Equation (4) for a 1-year period; ***using Equation (3) for a [SOURCE: Page_173]

246   1-year period. [SOURCE: Page_173]

247   Prescribed drugs varied with age group, with ORS prescribed to young children (‘< 5’) only, and [SOURCE: Page_173]

248   ‘Others’ preferentially prescribed to elders (> 65). However, the ORS and ‘Others’ groups [SOURCE: Page_173]

249   represent a very small proportion of AGE cases with prescriptions (Table 1). For these reasons, [SOURCE: Page_173]

14 [SOURCE: Page_173]

250   and because antiemetics are contraindicated for young children 44,45, we ignored the two ORS [SOURCE: Page_174]

251   groups and the ‘Others’ group when estimating the proportion of vomiting symptoms among the [SOURCE: Page_174]

252   AGE cases with prescriptions (Table 1). All the values obtained were higher than the probability [SOURCE: Page_174]

253   of vomiting for viral-etiology AGE �AGE|v (vom. ), which suggests viral infections concerned a [SOURCE: Page_174]

254   large proportion of all age groups. The shift from the sub-population with a prescription for AGE [SOURCE: Page_174]

255   to the entire AGE-sick population led to lower estimates of the proportion of vomiting [SOURCE: Page_174]

256   symptoms, which thus fell below the probability of vomiting for bacterial-etiology AGE [SOURCE: Page_174]

257   �AGE|b (vom. ) for the ‘< 5’ and ‘>65’ age-groups (Table 1). This suggests that most AGE did not [SOURCE: Page_174]

258   result from viral infections in these two groups. By contrast, the probabilities of vomiting for the [SOURCE: Page_174]

259   ‘5–65’ age group among the whole AGE-sick population led to an estimate of 100% of viral [SOURCE: Page_174]

260   cases within this group. As the contraindication of antiemetics for young children and the large- [SOURCE: Page_174]

261   scale use of ‘Others’ drugs for elders could have led to underestimate viral AGE cases in these [SOURCE: Page_174]

262   age groups, we preferred to work on the entire population, largely predominated by the ‘5-65’ [SOURCE: Page_174]

263   age group, regardless of age group. [SOURCE: Page_174]

264     Seasonal variations in probability of vomiting for the whole sick population were estimated [SOURCE: Page_174]

265   from seasonal variations in the corresponding probability of vomiting within the subset of people [SOURCE: Page_174]

266   with a prescription (Figures S3b-c). From these estimates, we deduced seasonal variations in the [SOURCE: Page_174]

267   probability of viral AGE and the number of viral AGE cases (Figures 1a-b). The numbers of new [SOURCE: Page_174]

268   viral AGE cases varied greatly from one day to the next. This strong variability resulted from the [SOURCE: Page_174]

269   variability in number of daily prescriptions (with deviations between consecutive days reaching [SOURCE: Page_174]

270   178 cases, with the biggest difference generally registered from Sunday to Monday) and from the [SOURCE: Page_174]

271   proportions of vomiting symptom that vary randomly in each of the populations suffering from [SOURCE: Page_174]

15 [SOURCE: Page_174]

272   viral or bacterial AGE (Supporting information). The 7-day sliding average greatly reduces this [SOURCE: Page_175]

273   variability (Figure 1b). [SOURCE: Page_175]

7­d Averages 285                                                       150.0 286 287                                                       100.0 288 289                                                        50.0 290 291                                                         0.0 292                                                               ­210   ­140   ­70      0    70     140    210 Julian Day 293 294   Figure 1a-b: Simulation from epidemiological data of (a) proportion of viral AGE, and (b) daily [SOURCE: Page_175]

295   number of viral AGE cases (Day 1: 1st January 2016). [SOURCE: Page_175]

296     Temporal trends in the estimated number of viral AGE cases were consistent with the [SOURCE: Page_175]

297   seasonality of viral AGE$_3$,50,51. Only EV (including coxsackievirus and echoviruses) have a [SOURCE: Page_175]

16 [SOURCE: Page_175]

298   summer maximum^52,53. In Poland, viruses account for 87% and 55% of viral or bacterial [SOURCE: Page_176]

299   gastroenteritis in February and July, respectively, with three times more viral AGE in winter than [SOURCE: Page_176]

300   in summer^50. Limits to our epidemiological analysis resulted from our working assumptions, [SOURCE: Page_176]

301   including the proportion of vomiting symptoms estimated without taking into account [SOURCE: Page_176]

302   prescriptions of ORS and ‘Others’, the retained proportions of vomiting symptoms for viral and [SOURCE: Page_176]

303   non-viral AGE cases, and the small size of the population involved. [SOURCE: Page_176]

304     Viral contaminations of wastewaters and Artière River water: WWTP efficiency. [SOURCE: Page_176]

305   All quantified viruses were almost systematically found in raw wastewaters, with the exception [SOURCE: Page_176]

306   of HAV which was never detected (Figure 2a). The non-detection of HAV could be explained by [SOURCE: Page_176]

307   the fact that it is rare in most European countries like France except after contaminations due to [SOURCE: Page_176]

308   international exchanges^54. Note however that HAV had sometimes been detected at the inlet of [SOURCE: Page_176]

309   the same WWTP in another study one year earlier 55. When above the detection thresholds, the [SOURCE: Page_176]

310   concentrations of other viruses ranged from (in GC.L-1) 10+3.1 to 10+7.0 for NVGI, 10+3.8 to 10+5.8 [SOURCE: Page_176]

311   for NVGII, 10+5.1 to 10+7.0 for RV-A, 10+3.8 to 10+5.2 for EV, and 10+5.8 to 10+10.0 for AdV [SOURCE: Page_176]

312   (Figure 2a). These recovery rates were equivalent to those reported in other European studies and [SOURCE: Page_176]

313   reflect wide virus circulation in the community^55–57. [SOURCE: Page_176]

314     When above the detection thresholds, the viral concentrations in treated wastewater at the [SOURCE: Page_176]

315   WWTP outlet (i.e. without additional tertiary treatment in lagoons) were one to a thousand times [SOURCE: Page_176]

316   less than in raw wastewater (results not shown). Detection in WWTP samples was much less [SOURCE: Page_176]

317   sensitive than that for river viruses due to the initial filtration of 1 L of water only. Thus, since [SOURCE: Page_176]

318   treated wastewater discharge contributed between 39 and 90% of the Artière River flow [SOURCE: Page_176]

319   downstream of their point of discharge, the flow of viruses in treated wastewater at the WWTP [SOURCE: Page_176]

320   outlet was better estimated from the increase in the amount of viruses carried by the Artière [SOURCE: Page_176]

17 [SOURCE: Page_176]

321   River between upstream and downstream of the wastewater discharge point plus wastewater [SOURCE: Page_177]

322   withdrawal for filling the lagoons minus virus flow resulting from WWTP by-pass. Virus [SOURCE: Page_177]

323   removal efficiency in the WWTP was then estimated by the ratio of virus flow between inlet and [SOURCE: Page_177]

324   outlet (Figure S$_4$). The annual geometric averages of virus removal within the WWTP over the [SOURCE: Page_177]

325   annual period 1st September 2015 – 31st August 2016 were 2.4, 1.9, 3.3, 2.6 and 2.5 log^10 for [SOURCE: Page_177]

326   NVGI, NVGII, RV-A, EV and AdV, respectively. These virus removals were a priori higher [SOURCE: Page_177]

327   than those measured in the WWTP of similar treatment trains 33,34,58 , which may be due to the [SOURCE: Page_177]

328   long residence time of wastewater in this treatment plant (2–3 days), the anoxic zone of the [SOURCE: Page_177]

329   activated sludge basins, and the use of iron chlorosulfate to precipitate phosphates during [SOURCE: Page_177]

330   clarification. Even though high variability between estimates led to uncertainties in the seasonal [SOURCE: Page_177]

331   variations (Figure S$_4$), the differences in log^10 removals between summer 2015 and winter 2016 [SOURCE: Page_177]

332   were 0.9, 2.7, 1.7 and 2.2 for NVGI, NVGII, EV and AdV, respectively. [SOURCE: Page_177]

333   While the Artière River generally only received treated wastewater from a small WWTP [SOURCE: Page_177]

334   (120 population equivalents) upstream of the discharge by the 'Trois Rivières' WWTP, virus [SOURCE: Page_177]

335   concentrations upstream were sometimes of similar order of magnitude to virus concentrations [SOURCE: Page_177]

336   downstream of the 'Trois Rivières' WWTP discharge point (Figures 2b-c), as it was possible for [SOURCE: Page_177]

337   wastewater to discharge directly from the sewer system upstream of the 'Trois Rivières' WWTP. [SOURCE: Page_177]

338   When above the detection thresholds, virus concentrations in the Artière River downstream of [SOURCE: Page_177]

339   the WWTP discharge ranged from (in GC.L-1) 10+1.6 to 10+4.1 for NVGI, 10+0.8 to 10+3.3 for [SOURCE: Page_177]

340   NVGII, 10+2.8 to 10+4.0 for RV-A, 10+0.8 to 10+2.4 for EV, and 10+2.8 to 10+6.0 for AdV (Figure [SOURCE: Page_177]

341   2c). These orders of magnitude were in agreement with published data^2–4, and with the seasonal [SOURCE: Page_177]

342   variations in quantity^59,60. [SOURCE: Page_177]

371     Comparing the simulated shedding and environmental fate of viruses to the [SOURCE: Page_179]

372   weighted sum of the virus flows monitored in situ. The simulated quantities of daily [SOURCE: Page_179]

373   virus shedding were compared to the weighted sums of the flows of viruses monitored at the inlet [SOURCE: Page_179]

375   NVGII, RV-A, EV and AdV, respectively. There was good agreement between simulated trends [SOURCE: Page_179]

378   3). Note that qPCR measurements have high uncertainties, making their use more appropriate for [SOURCE: Page_179]

379   variations over several powers of 10 than for variations of viral AGE numbers of less than 10 [SOURCE: Page_179]

387     NVGI and EV did not contribute to the equivalent virus concentrations (i.e. a = d = 0), whereas [SOURCE: Page_180]

388   the relative contributions of the other three viruses to the equivalent virus concentrations [SOURCE: Page_180]

389   decreased in the order NVGII (b = 10-2) > RV-A (c =5.8 10-4) > AdV (e = 4.6 10-5). The lower [SOURCE: Page_180]

390   concentrations of EV compared to other viruses could explain why d = 0. For the other more [SOURCE: Page_180]

391   abundant viruses, their specific weighting coefficients should a priori be proportional to the [SOURCE: Page_180]

392   “number of AGE cases-to-quantity of shed viruses for all carriers” ratio for a given virus. Since [SOURCE: Page_180]

393   some of the monitored viruses may cause AGE symptoms other than the vomiting and/or [SOURCE: Page_180]

394   diarrhea, carriers without AGE symptoms should be distinguished from those with AGE [SOURCE: Page_180]

395   symptoms, rather than distinguishing asymptomatic from symptomatic carriers as often done. [SOURCE: Page_180]

396     Virus concentrations in the Artière River downstream wastewater discharge were simulated by [SOURCE: Page_180]

397   mixing water coming from the Artière River upstream of the wastewater discharge, treated [SOURCE: Page_180]

398   wastewater discharged into the Artière River (i.e. not withdrawn for filling lagoons), and raw [SOURCE: Page_180]

399   wastewater discharged without treatment through the WWTP bypass. For this purpose, virus [SOURCE: Page_180]

400   removal in the WWTP was simulated in each basin zone with Q 10 = 1.7 (Supporting [SOURCE: Page_180]

401   Information). Simulated virus concentrations were compared to equivalent virus concentrations [SOURCE: Page_180]

402   using the weighting coefficient values already estimated from virus flow in raw wastewaters [SOURCE: Page_180]

403   (Figure 4). There was good agreement between simulated trends and weighted observations, with [SOURCE: Page_180]

404   the exception of a very high weighted observation on September 1st, 2015 and two consecutive [SOURCE: Page_180]

405   zero values between early April 2016 and early May 2016 (Figure 4). A bypass of 320 m³ was [SOURCE: Page_180]

406   operated on September 1st, 2015, the day when the weighted observations corresponded to a [SOURCE: Page_180]

407   concentration in significantly higher equivalent viruses (6 10+5 viruses.L-1) than the value [SOURCE: Page_180]

408   simulated from epidemiological data (1.9 10+2 viruses.L-1). At this date, it is likely that the by- [SOURCE: Page_180]

21 [SOURCE: Page_180]

409   pass (very low compared to other dates) was operated during less than 1 hour concomitantly to [SOURCE: Page_181]

410   our water sampling, leading to a higher spot contamination of the Artière River than the [SOURCE: Page_181]

411   simulated value assuming that the by-pass was operated over 24 hours. Two other peaks reaching [SOURCE: Page_181]

412   about 1.5 10+6 virus.L-1 were simulated using epidemiological data on two other days with a [SOURCE: Page_181]

413   daily by-pass that could reach 10500 m^3.day-1 (more than 30 times the by-pass flow of 1st [SOURCE: Page_181]

414   September 2015). Beyond this spot difference, the simulated results show how much operation [SOURCE: Page_181]

415   of the by-pass can occasionally (but recurrently) affect contamination of Artière River (Figure 4). [SOURCE: Page_181]

434 435     Thus, our model combining AGE epidemiology, viral excretion and subsequent environmental [SOURCE: Page_182]

436   fate of human pathogenic enteric viruses makes it possible to simulate the main trends observed. [SOURCE: Page_182]

437   This model can thus serve to compare treatment, discharge and reuse scenarios. Limits in the [SOURCE: Page_182]

438   comparison between simulations and weighted experimental data resulted from some of the [SOURCE: Page_182]

439   model-simplifying assumptions (e.g. virus removal neglected in the sewerage system, additional [SOURCE: Page_182]

440   water sources and sinks in the Artière River) and experimental limits, including other AGE- [SOURCE: Page_182]

441   related viruses (e.g. aichiviruses and astroviruses)3 that were not quantified [SOURCE: Page_182]

442 [SOURCE: Page_182]

## VARIABILITY OF VOMITING SYMPTOM

Uncertainties in the number of daily new viral AGE cases resulted from the variability in [SOURCE: Page_194]

the number of daily consultations and the proportions of vomiting symptoms in the population [SOURCE: Page_194]

suffering from viral or bacterial AGE. We assumed that the probabilities to vomit were 0.52 [SOURCE: Page_194]

and 0.66 for each AGE case of bacterial and viral etiologies, respectively. For each of 50, 200 [SOURCE: Page_194]

and 1000 people suffering from AGE sets and for each of viral AGE proportions varying from [SOURCE: Page_194]

0 to 1 in steps of 0.1, we performed 50 Monte Carlo simulations (Figures S$_2$). [SOURCE: Page_194]

Figures S2a-c: proportions of viral AGE estimated from the actual viral AGE proportions for [SOURCE: Page_195]

Monte Carlo simulations, assuming that vomiting probabilities were 0.52 and 0.66 for viral [SOURCE: Page_195]

3 [SOURCE: Page_195]

The uncertainty in the resulting estimated proportion of viral AGE decreased with increasing [SOURCE: Page_196]

the total number of AGE cases. Overestimations and underestimations of the proportion of [SOURCE: Page_196]

viral AGE were noted for actual proportions of viral AGE near 0 and 1, respectively, but they [SOURCE: Page_196]

decreased with increasing the total number of AGE cases. The use of sliding averages over 7 [SOURCE: Page_196]

days greatly minimized the uncertainties resulting from the random nature of the vomiting [SOURCE: Page_196]

symptom (Figure 1b of the research article). [SOURCE: Page_196]

3. SEASONAL VARIATIONS IN THE PROBABILITY OF VOMITING FOR THE WHOLE POPULATION Seasonal variations in the probability of vomiting for the whole population were estimated [SOURCE: Page_196]

from seasonal variations in the probability of vomiting within the subset of sick people [SOURCE: Page_196]

consulting a physician (Figures S3a-c). Differences between the three age groups '< 5', '5-65' [SOURCE: Page_196]

and '> 65' in AGE or symptoms could help to better identify the potential origins of the AGE. [SOURCE: Page_196]

For example, the increase at the end of 2015 in the number of AGE cases in the '5-65' age [SOURCE: Page_196]

group probably resulted from the consumption by this group of raw oysters during Christmas [SOURCE: Page_196]

time. [SOURCE: Page_196]

4. VIRUS REMOVALS IN THE 'TROIS RIVIÈRES' WWTP; MEASUREMENTS AND SIMULATIONS Virus removals (i.e. virus inflow – to – virus outflow ratio or its decimal logarithm) within [SOURCE: Page_196]

the WWTP were estimated for each of the quantified viruses at each sampling date. Virus [SOURCE: Page_196]

inflows were estimated from measured wastewater flow and virus concentrations within raw [SOURCE: Page_196]

wastewaters. Virus outflows could be estimated from either (i) virus concentrations in treated [SOURCE: Page_196]

wastewaters, or (ii) the increases in virus flow in the Artière River resulting from treated [SOURCE: Page_196]

wastewater discharge, taking into account any by-pass of raw wastewater and/or treated [SOURCE: Page_196]

## 1.E+00

DATE Figure S$_4$: NVGI and NVG$_2$ removals at each sampling date. [SOURCE: Page_198]

The geometric averages of virus removals within the WWTP over the annual period 1 st [SOURCE: Page_198]

September 2015 – 31st August 2016 were 2.4, 1.9, 3.3, 2.6 and 2.5 log^10 for the NVGI, [SOURCE: Page_198]

NVGII, RV-A, EV and AdV, respectively. The high variability between estimates led to [SOURCE: Page_198]

uncertainties in the seasonal variations; nonetheless, the differences of log 10 removals [SOURCE: Page_198]

between summer 2015 and winter 2016 were 0.9, 2.7, 1.7 and 2.2 for NVGI, NVGII, EV and [SOURCE: Page_198]

AdV, respectively. We checked whether these orders of magnitude could be explained [SOURCE: Page_198]

assuming seasonal variations in wastewater flow and temperature (Figure S$_5$). [SOURCE: Page_198]

## TEMPERATURE (T°C) 20

15 [SOURCE: Page_199]

Treated Wastewater 10 ­210   ­140   ­70   0      70      140   210 DATE [SOURCE: Page_199]

Figure S$_5$: Seasonal variations in wastewater temperature at the outlet of the treatment plant. [SOURCE: Page_199]

The treatment train of the 'Trois Rivières' WWTP combines a pre-treatment with screening, [SOURCE: Page_199]

degreasing and sand sedimentation, an activated sludge treatment with anoxic, hypoxic and [SOURCE: Page_199]

aerated zones, and a clarification using iron chlorosulfate to precipitate phosphates and [SOURCE: Page_199]

organic polymers to precipitate the activated sludge. Virus removal within the 'Trois Rivières' [SOURCE: Page_199]

WWTP was thus simulated by considering removals successively within the primary settling [SOURCE: Page_199]

tank, the anoxic, hypoxic and aerated zones of the activated sludge tanks, and the clarifiers. In [SOURCE: Page_199]

each of these compartments, we assumed the following equation: [SOURCE: Page_199]

�� �t × ��o = (�i − �o ) × �w − �t (�) × �t × �o                        (S$_1$) [SOURCE: Page_199]

where Vt is the volume of the considered tank (m^3), Ci and Co the virus concentrations (GC L- 1 ) at the inlet and outlet of the tank, respectively, t the time (hour), Q w the water flow (m^3 h-1), [SOURCE: Page_199]

and kt the tank removal rate (h-1) depending on the temperature T (°C) of wastewater. Co is [SOURCE: Page_199]

also the virus concentration within the tank. [SOURCE: Page_199]

We set kt(T) values at 20°C to 0.1, 0.6, 0.4, 0.4 and 0.1 hour-1 for the primary settling tank, the [SOURCE: Page_199]

anoxic, hypoxic and aerated zones of the activated sludge tank, and the clarifiers, respectively. [SOURCE: Page_199]

7 [SOURCE: Page_199]

We assumed that variations in the removal rates with temperatures could be described by a [SOURCE: Page_200]

Q10 equation: �−20 �t (�) = �t (20°�) × �10 10 (S$_2$) [SOURCE: Page_200]

where Q10 is the proportional increase of kt with 10°C increase in temperature. We assumed [SOURCE: Page_200]

that Q10=1.7. The mean temperature T of all the tanks of the WWTP were assumed equal to [SOURCE: Page_200]

the temperature measured in the treated wastewater outflow. For each season, we present in [SOURCE: Page_200]

Figure S$_6$ examples over 48 hours periods without by-pass. [SOURCE: Page_200]

4 Sampling time VIRUS REMOVAL (LOG10) [SOURCE: Page_200]

3 [SOURCE: Page_200]

autumn (November 6-8 2015), winter (January 18-20 2016), spring (April 6-8 2015) and [SOURCE: Page_200]

summer (August 11-13 2015). [SOURCE: Page_200]

The daily virus removal within the WWTP over the 1 st September 2015 – 31st August 2016 [SOURCE: Page_200]

period was simulated by simultaneously using this simplified modelling and taking into [SOURCE: Page_200]

account wastewater bypass that explained some days most of Artière viral contamination [SOURCE: Page_200]

(Fig. 4 of the research article). [SOURCE: Page_200]

8 [SOURCE: Page_200]

treated wastewater samplings) varied hourly with 0.5 log^10 differences between minimum and [SOURCE: Page_201]

maximum values (Fig. S$_6$). We noted seasonal variations with mean removal rates of 2.9, 2.2, [SOURCE: Page_201]

2.5 and 3.3 for the considered periods in autumn, winter, spring and summer seasons, [SOURCE: Page_201]

respectively. In addition for simultaneous raw and treated wastewater samplings at about 10 h [SOURCE: Page_201]

a.m. UT, the apparent virus removals exceed the daily averages of about 0.2, 0.3, 0.1 and 0.2 [SOURCE: Page_201]

Modelling the removal and reversible Annexe IV. [SOURCE: Page_205]
