*        Cette Annexe correspond au Chapitre III de la présente thèse tel qu’il a été soumis à European Journal of Soil Science. Il contient le manuscrit de l’article accompagné des figures et Supporting Informations. [SOURCE: Page_205]

1 [SOURCE: Page_205]

Modelling the removal and reversible immobilization of murine noroviruses in a [SOURCE: Page_206]

phaeozem under various contamination and rinsing conditions [SOURCE: Page_206]

V. T E S S ON a, A. D E R O UG EM ON T b,c, L. C A P O W IE Z a, P. R EN AU L T a [SOURCE: Page_206]

Correspondence: P. Renault. E-mail: [EMAIL_REDACTED] [SOURCE: Page_206]

a INRA/UAPV, UMR 1114 EMMAH, 228 route de l’Aérodrome, CS 40 509, 84914 Avignon [SOURCE: Page_206]

Cedex 9, France, bCNR des Virus des Gastro-Entérites, 2 rue Angélique Ducoudray, [SOURCE: Page_206]

BP37013, 21070 Dijon Cedex, France, and cUniversité de Bourgogne/AgroSup Dijon, UMR [SOURCE: Page_206]

PAM A 02.102, 7 boulevard Jeanne d'Arc, 21000 Dijon, France [SOURCE: Page_206]

Running title: Modelling the fate of noroviruses in a phaeozem [SOURCE: Page_206]

Summary [SOURCE: Page_206]

Enteric viruses entering the soil with contaminated irrigation water can reach groundwater or [SOURCE: Page_206]

be internalized in plants via their roots without being inactivated. Their fate in the soil [SOURCE: Page_206]

depends on the virus, the soil and the soil solution. In order to write a mathematical model [SOURCE: Page_206]

suitable for a calcaric phaeozem, we investigated murine norovirus removal and reversible [SOURCE: Page_206]

immobilization in aggregate columns according to saturation procedure, conditions between [SOURCE: Page_206]

times of contamination and rinsing, temperature, and soil solution. Viruses were quantified [SOURCE: Page_206]

before and after 0.45 µm filtration using RT-qPCR. Experimental results supported a model [SOURCE: Page_206]

combining free and colloidal transport of viruses in mobile water, exchange of free viruses [SOURCE: Page_206]

between mobile and immobile waters, virus removal, and reversible virus adsorptions on [SOURCE: Page_206]

2 [SOURCE: Page_206]

suspended colloids, aggregate outer surface and particles within aggregates. For artificial soil [SOURCE: Page_207]

solution at 20°C, the fate of viruses in contaminations lasting 1 to 7 days followed by 7 hours [SOURCE: Page_207]

rinsing could be described by combining 0.38 log^10 daily removal and weak reversible [SOURCE: Page_207]

immobilization using a Freundlich isotherm (kF = 1120, nF = 1.53), which explained why free [SOURCE: Page_207]

viruses prevailed in mobile water. Partial drying without aggregate desaturation did not affect [SOURCE: Page_207]

virus recovery. Mg$_2$+ enrichment induced geochemical changes that faded over time, resulting [SOURCE: Page_207]

in up to ten times more viruses adsorbed on suspended colloids than free, and enhanced [SOURCE: Page_207]

adsorption on aggregate outer surfaces; similarly, groundwater slowed remobilization. The [SOURCE: Page_207]

fate of murine norovirus within a calcaric phaeozem can be described by a model that takes [SOURCE: Page_207]

into account geochemical fluctuations. [SOURCE: Page_207]

Key words: wastewater reuse, enteric virus, soil, virus fate, geochemical changes, simulation [SOURCE: Page_207]

Acknowledgments [SOURCE: Page_207]

This work was supported by the French national research agency under the "Investments for [SOURCE: Page_207]

the Future" program, reference ANR-10-LABX-001-01 Labex Agro (Full project No. 1403- [SOURCE: Page_207]

050 "Fate of human enteric viruses after the discharge or reuse of treated wastewater"). We [SOURCE: Page_207]

thank the Council of the Provence-Alpes-Côte-d'Azur Region and the French National [SOURCE: Page_207]

Institute of Agronomic Research (INRA) for support through V. Tesson's PhD grant. We [SOURCE: Page_207]

thank H.W. Virgin (University of Washington, MO, US) who kindly provided the CW$_1$ strain [SOURCE: Page_207]

of murine norovirus (MNV-1), and the ATCC (Manassas, VA, US) who supplied the Centre [SOURCE: Page_207]

National de Référence des Virus Entériques in RAW 264.7 mouse leukemic monocyte [SOURCE: Page_207]

macrophages (ATCC® TIB-71TM). We thank Fabien Hubert (IC2MP, Poitiers University, [SOURCE: Page_207]

France) for X-ray characterization of our soil, and Hervé Gaillard (UR SOL, INRA, Orléans) [SOURCE: Page_207]

3 [SOURCE: Page_207]

for aggregate bulk density measurements. Lastly, we thank Richard Ryan for English editing [SOURCE: Page_208]

and proofreading. [SOURCE: Page_208]

Highlights [SOURCE: Page_208]

   How far does soil reduce the risk that noroviruses brought by irrigation reach [SOURCE: Page_208]

groundwater or roots? [SOURCE: Page_208]

   Virus removal over a five-day average residence time in phaeozem is about two log^10. [SOURCE: Page_208]

   Reversible virus immobilization is weak; Mg intake favours it, together with colloidal [SOURCE: Page_208]

transport. [SOURCE: Page_208]

   Virus removal and reversible adsorption may be modelled to better assess the risk of [SOURCE: Page_208]

contamination. [SOURCE: Page_208]

4 [SOURCE: Page_208]

Introduction [SOURCE: Page_209]

Wastewater reuse for crop irrigation can help address increasing water scarcity and declining [SOURCE: Page_209]

conventional water quality by upgrading marginal fertilizing resources and curbing their [SOURCE: Page_209]

discharge in conventional water bodies (Jaramillo & Restrepo, 2017). However, their [SOURCE: Page_209]

chemical and microbial contents imply environmental, agricultural and health risks. Domestic [SOURCE: Page_209]

wastewaters generally contain human enteric viruses (Jaramillo & Restrepo, 2017) that have [SOURCE: Page_209]

often been identified as causing waterborne and foodborne disease outbreaks in industrialized [SOURCE: Page_209]

countries (Kirk et al., 2015). Noroviruses, reportedly responsible for 36% and 67% of [SOURCE: Page_209]

gastroenteritis in the world and in Europe, respectively (Kirk et al., 2015), are by far the [SOURCE: Page_209]

leading cause of outbreaks associated with the consumption of leafy vegetables in the USA [SOURCE: Page_209]

(Herman et al., 2015). Their environmental transmission is favoured by strong excretion over [SOURCE: Page_209]

weeks by both sick and healthy carriers (Teunis et al., 2015), resistance to conventional water [SOURCE: Page_209]

treatments, persistence in the environment for several weeks to months (Jaramillo & Restrepo, [SOURCE: Page_209]

2017), and low infectious dose (Atmar et al., 2014). [SOURCE: Page_209]

During irrigation by domestic wastewater, human enteric viruses can be dispersed as [SOURCE: Page_209]

bioaerosols or deposited at the surface of the soil and plants. Viruses can then disseminate [SOURCE: Page_209]

from the soil surface to the aquifer (Murphy et al., 2017) or be internalized in plants via their [SOURCE: Page_209]

roots, and then disseminate to their edible portions without being inactivated (DiCaprio et al., [SOURCE: Page_209]

2015). In the soil, virus fate combines transport, immobilization and inactivation [SOURCE: Page_209]

(Bhattacharjee et al., 2002). These processes are interdependent: viruses are transferred either [SOURCE: Page_209]

free or adsorbed on colloids (Syngouna & Chrysikopoulos, 2010), and virus inactivation may [SOURCE: Page_209]

be slowed by their immobilization in biofilms (Skraber et al., 2005) or accelerated by their [SOURCE: Page_209]

adsorption on metal oxides at solution pH close to neutrality (Zhuang & Jin, 2008). [SOURCE: Page_209]

5 [SOURCE: Page_209]

Virus immobilization depends on the species, strain and conformation of capsid proteins, [SOURCE: Page_210]

on soil minerals and organic compounds, and on the soil solution (Dowd et al., 1998; Zhuang [SOURCE: Page_210]

& Jin, 2008; Cao et al., 2010; Michen & Graule, 2010; da Silva et al., 2011). When they are [SOURCE: Page_210]

small enough, like noroviruses (27 nm), in contrast to bacteriophage PM$_2$ (60 nm), and not [SOURCE: Page_210]

aggregated by pH near their isoelectric point (Michen & Graule, 2010), their immobilization [SOURCE: Page_210]

results from adsorption rather than trapping in pore constrictions or surface crevices (Dowd et [SOURCE: Page_210]

al., 1998). Forces involved in virus adsorption include Van der Waals, electrical, hydrophobic [SOURCE: Page_210]

and Born forces (Park & Kim, 2015). Whereas Van der Waals forces are always attractive, [SOURCE: Page_210]

whether the electrostatic forces are repulsive or attractive depends on virus and soil particle [SOURCE: Page_210]

charges, which vary with the pH and ionic composition of the solution (Cao et al., 2010). A [SOURCE: Page_210]

few solids, such as Fe oxides, have high isoelectric points that favour virus adsorption over a [SOURCE: Page_210]

wider pH range (Syngouna & Chrysikopoulos, 2010). Adsorption is favoured by the presence [SOURCE: Page_210]

of divalent cations (Cao et al., 2010; da Silva et al., 2011). Soluble organic compounds may [SOURCE: Page_210]

compete with viruses for adsorption (Cao et al., 2010), while solid organic compounds may [SOURCE: Page_210]

increase sites on which viruses may be immobilized (Schijven & Hassanizadeh, 2000). [SOURCE: Page_210]

Hydrophobic forces are often assumed to result from Lewis acid-base interactions (van Oss, [SOURCE: Page_210]

1993); they vary with the virus (Dika et al., 2013), and are reported to increase with [SOURCE: Page_210]

temperature (Syngouna & Chrysikopoulos, 2010). [SOURCE: Page_210]

Although some models of virus immobilization in soil have been part of more integrative [SOURCE: Page_210]

models of virus fate in soil or the vadoze zone, with only cursory experimental validations if [SOURCE: Page_210]

any (Bhattacharjee et al., 2002), other models are supported by laboratory experiments on [SOURCE: Page_210]

immobilization (Tesson & Renault, 2017). Moving viruses have been assumed to be either [SOURCE: Page_210]

exclusively free (Sasidharan et al., 2017) or adsorbed on dispersed colloids (Mayotte et al., [SOURCE: Page_210]

2017); in this last case, inert colloid immobilization was assumed to result mainly from [SOURCE: Page_210]

filtration (Yao et al., 1971). Virus immobilization has sometimes been held to be irreversible [SOURCE: Page_210]

6 [SOURCE: Page_210]

(Praetorius et al., 2014), as suggested by the colloid filtration theory (Yao et al., 1971) and [SOURCE: Page_211]

the extended DLVO theory if the distance between viruses and soil particles corresponds to [SOURCE: Page_211]

the primary minimum of energy (Praetorius et al., 2014). More often, virus immobilization [SOURCE: Page_211]

has been held to be reversible (Cao et al., 2010; Sasidharan et al., 2017), and explained either [SOURCE: Page_211]

by a distance between virus and soil particle corresponding to the secondary minimum of [SOURCE: Page_211]

energy, when this minimum exists (Sadeghi et al., 2013), or by geochemical changes that [SOURCE: Page_211]

modify energy profile (Cao et al., 2010). Simultaneous reversible and irreversible [SOURCE: Page_211]

immobilizations have also been described by distinguishing adsorption sites (Cao et al., 2010) [SOURCE: Page_211]

or simulating the kinetic inactivation of reversibly adsorbed viruses (Tufenkji & Elimelech, [SOURCE: Page_211]

2004). Reversible virus adsorption has been kinetically described by introducing attachment [SOURCE: Page_211]

and detachment kinetic coefficients (Sasidharan et al., 2017); other works have described [SOURCE: Page_211]

equilibrium between viruses in solution and viruses adsorbed on solids with partition [SOURCE: Page_211]

coefficient KD (Syngouna & Chrysikopoulos, 2010), Freundlich or Langmuir isotherms [SOURCE: Page_211]

(Vilker & Burge, 1980). However, to our knowledge, no study has dealt with reversible virus [SOURCE: Page_211]

immobilization and/or irreversible removal for real soils containing more than 35% clays [SOURCE: Page_211]

(Tesson & Renault, 2017). [SOURCE: Page_211]

We set out to collect new experimental data for a calcaric phaeozem (FAO classification), [SOURCE: Page_211]

and write a suitable mathematical model, i.e. considering kinetic exchanges or only [SOURCE: Page_211]

equilibrium between suspended and reversibly adsorbed viruses (partition coefficient, [SOURCE: Page_211]

Freundlich isotherms or Langmuir isotherms). [SOURCE: Page_211]

Materials and Methods [SOURCE: Page_211]

Virus, soil and artificial soil solution [SOURCE: Page_211]

7 [SOURCE: Page_211]

The cytopathic CW$_1$ strain of murine norovirus (MNV-1), as a surrogate of human enteric [SOURCE: Page_212]

noroviruses, was kindly provided by H.W. Virgin (University of Washington, MO, US). [SOURCE: Page_212]

Noroviruses were propagated on RAW 264.7 mouse leukemic monocyte macrophages [SOURCE: Page_212]

(ATCC® TIB-71TM) as previously described (Wobus et al., 2004). Two days after infection, [SOURCE: Page_212]

the cells and their culture medium were collected, frozen in liquid nitrogen and thawed three [SOURCE: Page_212]

times, and centrifuged (10 minutes at 1000 g then 30 minutes at 5900 g). The supernatant was [SOURCE: Page_212]

transferred to Amicon® Ultra-15 tangential filters (Merck Millipore LTD, Ireland) and [SOURCE: Page_212]

centrifuged for 40 minutes at 1000 g. The pellet was resuspended in 1 ml of phosphate- [SOURCE: Page_212]

buffered saline (PBS) to obtain approximately 1013.1 genomic copies (GC) ml-1. The [SOURCE: Page_212]

concentrated viral suspension was frozen in liquid nitrogen and stored at −80°C, later re- [SOURCE: Page_212]

aliquoted into 10 µl ready-to-use aliquots, refrozen in liquid nitrogen and stored again at [SOURCE: Page_212]

−80°C until experiments began. For each column experiment, one 10 µl aliquot was thawed at [SOURCE: Page_212]

room temperature, directly diluted with 40 ml of artificial soil solution, and homogenized for [SOURCE: Page_212]

30 s. Viruses were quantified by RT-qPCR. Practically, 60 µl of viral RNA was extracted [SOURCE: Page_212]

from 140 µl of sample following the manufacturer’s instructions (QIAamp® Viral RNA kit [SOURCE: Page_212]

(Qiagen)), and immediately purified with One-Step™ PCR Inhibitor Removal (Zymo [SOURCE: Page_212]

Research Corp, CA) according to the manufacturer’s protocol. RT-qPCR was performed as [SOURCE: Page_212]

previously described (Belliot et al., 2008) using a Mx^3005P® qPCR machine (Agilent [SOURCE: Page_212]

Technologies). For a homogeneous virus suspension, the detection threshold of the RT-qPCR [SOURCE: Page_212]

analyzes was about 350 GC ml-1, and the standard deviation of the decimal logarithm of [SOURCE: Page_212]

measurement replicates was always lower than 0.1. [SOURCE: Page_212]

The 0–15 cm surface layer of calcaric phaeozem (FAO classification) was sampled in the [SOURCE: Page_212]

French Limagne Noire, a small region near Clermont-Ferrand (45.85874 N, 3.20088 E). [SOURCE: Page_212]

Average annual rainfall was 566 mm for the period 2010–2016. The field where sampling [SOURCE: Page_212]

took place had been irrigated yearly with treated wastewater from the wastewater treatment [SOURCE: Page_212]

8 [SOURCE: Page_212]

plant of Clermont-Auvergne-Metropole after an additional lagooning tertiary treatment. It had [SOURCE: Page_213]

been cultivated with sugar beet since 2015 and the soil was bare, recently tilled but unsown at [SOURCE: Page_213]

sampling on 5 January 2016. The soil was stored until use at room temperature at residual [SOURCE: Page_213]

moisture of 6.9% dry weight basis. The properties of the 0–15 cm layer were: 3 g kg-1 CaCO$_3$, [SOURCE: Page_213]

and after decarbonation, 559 g kg-1 clay, 222 g kg-1 silt, 216 g kg-1 sand, 16 5 g kg-1 organic [SOURCE: Page_213]

C, 1.58 g kg-1 total N, 12.7 mg kg-1 N-NH$_4$+, 29.9 mg kg-1 N-NO$_3$-. Soil pH(water) was 8.16. [SOURCE: Page_213]

Its cation exchange capacity (NF ISO 23470) was 0.465 molc kg-1 soil, and the concentrations [SOURCE: Page_213]

of exchangeable Ca$_2$+, Mg$_2$+, Na+, K+ extracted by cobaltihexamine were 0.364, 0.0625, [SOURCE: Page_213]

0.00625, 0.011 molc kg-1 soil, respectively. Interstratified clays (illite-smectite and illite- [SOURCE: Page_213]

smectite-chlorite) were the dominant clays; their behaviour was affected by complex with [SOURCE: Page_213]

organic matter (Bornand et al., 1984). The soil probably contained between 4.5 and 6.1% iron [SOURCE: Page_213]

(Bornand et al., 1984); 3–4 mm aggregate bulk density was 2.0 g ml-1. [SOURCE: Page_213]

Artificial soil solution was prepared initially from a mixture of ultrapure water, and the [SOURCE: Page_213]

soil sampled one year later (5:1 w/v), mixed for 30 minutes, and centrifuged at 10 000 g for [SOURCE: Page_213]

30 minutes to recover supernatant, which was filtered through NanoCeram® filters. Soil [SOURCE: Page_213]

solution was then autoclaved, aliquoted in 40 ml tubes and stored at 4°C until use. Just before [SOURCE: Page_213]

the experiment, some of the 40 mL aliquots of artificial solutions were optionally enriched [SOURCE: Page_213]

with the equivalent of 10 mM MgCl$_2$ or 1.25 mg of fulvic acids (1R105F Nordic Aquatic [SOURCE: Page_213]

Fulvic Acid Reference, IHSS). The properties of the artificial soil solution, as well as those [SOURCE: Page_213]

for groundwater and wastewater are reported in Table S$_1$ (Supporting Information). [SOURCE: Page_213]

Experimental design and procedure [SOURCE: Page_213]

In order to address the partition of viruses in solution between free viruses and viruses [SOURCE: Page_213]

adsorbed on dispersed colloids, the reversible immobilization of viruses on undispersed soil [SOURCE: Page_213]

9 [SOURCE: Page_213]

and the removal of viruses due to their irreversible immobilization or to their destruction, it is [SOURCE: Page_214]

interesting to vary the conditions that affect soil colloid dispersion (time elapsed after soil [SOURCE: Page_214]

wetting, water chemistry), and electrical and hydrophobic forces involved in the adsorption of [SOURCE: Page_214]

viruses on soil particles and at air-water interfaces (water chemistry for electrical forces, [SOURCE: Page_214]

temperature for hydrophobic forces). Moreover, comparison of virus fate for different soil [SOURCE: Page_214]

structures could help assessing the impact of virus transfer on the observed kinetics. [SOURCE: Page_214]

The experimental design therefore combined experiments on immobilization for batches [SOURCE: Page_214]

(soil dispersed in stirred solutions) and columns of soil aggregates, with experiments on the [SOURCE: Page_214]

possible remobilizing of viruses previously removed from the solution (Figure S$_1$, Supporting [SOURCE: Page_214]

Information). Conditions that were varied between the experiments were the initial column [SOURCE: Page_214]

saturation procedure (i.e. under partial vacuum or at atmospheric air pressure), saturation of [SOURCE: Page_214]

the column just before or well before contamination of the circulating solution, temperature, [SOURCE: Page_214]

soil moisture, and chemical conditions. [SOURCE: Page_214]

For experiments on virus immobilization, columns (inner diameter and height 18 mm and [SOURCE: Page_214]

3.6 mm, respectively; total volume 9.2 ml) were initially filled with about 8 g of 3–4 mm or [SOURCE: Page_214]

4–5 mm aggregates of soil at residual moisture, and saturated with the artificial soil solution [SOURCE: Page_214]

either under vacuum (0.01 MPa air) or in air to limit or favour the trapping of air bubbles. It [SOURCE: Page_214]

results in about 2.65 ml of solid, 0.93 ml of intra-aggregate pores and 5.62 ml of inter- [SOURCE: Page_214]

aggregate pores. The solutions circulated in closed circuits between the columns and [SOURCE: Page_214]

reservoirs initially filled with 40 ml of the same artificial soil solution at a flow rate of about [SOURCE: Page_214]

4 ml minute-1 using a peristaltic pump (Figure 1 and Figures S2a-b, Supporting Information). [SOURCE: Page_214]

Approximately 2 ml of water was contained in the tubes between the soil column and the [SOURCE: Page_214]

reservoir. The contamination (at time t = 0) followed the initial saturation of the column by [SOURCE: Page_214]

about 15 minutes or 17 hours depending on whether the quantity of suspended soil colloids, [SOURCE: Page_214]

expected to promote transient virus transfer, was to be maximized or minimized. At about 0, [SOURCE: Page_214]

10 [SOURCE: Page_214]

0.25, 0.5, 1, 2, 5, 7 and 24 hours after the contamination, 1.5 ml of soil solution was sampled [SOURCE: Page_215]

in the reservoir; 1 ml was filtered at 0.45 µm, and the filtered and unfiltered sampled solutions [SOURCE: Page_215]

were distributed in 140 μl, 200 μl and 160 μl for RT-qPCR, cell culture and precautionary [SOURCE: Page_215]

storage, respectively. There was only one replicate per date and experiment. [SOURCE: Page_215]

For virus remobilization, some of the columns previously contaminated were first stored [SOURCE: Page_215]

for different times and moisture conditions, and then rinsed by virus-free soil solutions at a [SOURCE: Page_215]

flow rate of about 4 ml minute-1 by creating an open circuit with the reservoir and the column [SOURCE: Page_215]

in series (Figure 1). At about 0, 0.17, 0.33, 0.5, 1, 2, 5, 7 hours, 1.5 ml of soil solution was [SOURCE: Page_215]

sampled at the column outlet. [SOURCE: Page_215]

Batch incubations were performed in sealed 50-millilitre tubes initially filled with 4 g of [SOURCE: Page_215]

air-dried soil and 22.75 ml of the soil solution to have the same solution-to-soil ratio as in the [SOURCE: Page_215]

column experiments. The tubes were continuously shaken in darkness for 6 hours. They were [SOURCE: Page_215]

inoculated 120, 90, 60, 30, 15, 8, 4, 2 minutes before batch ending with 5 µl of viral [SOURCE: Page_215]

suspension to have the same initial contamination per mass of soil as in the column [SOURCE: Page_215]

experiments. One tube without soil was inoculated and immediately centrifuged to obtain a [SOURCE: Page_215]

zero time point. The tubes were then centrifuged at 10 000 g for 30 minutes, and the [SOURCE: Page_215]

supernatant was aliquoted as described above. [SOURCE: Page_215]

At the end of the immobilization experiments and after centrifuging the tubes in the batch [SOURCE: Page_215]

experiments, the soil solutions were sampled for subsequent analyses at the Laboratoire [SOURCE: Page_215]

d'Analyses des Sols (Arras, France): pH, organic C, N-NH$_4$+, Ca$_2$+, Mg$_2$+, K+, Na+. [SOURCE: Page_215]

Process modelling and experimental data analysis [SOURCE: Page_215]

We neglected the volumes of water in the tubes connecting the reservoirs to the soil columns. [SOURCE: Page_215]

11 [SOURCE: Page_215]

For experiments on virus immobilizations with water flowing in a closed loop between [SOURCE: Page_216]

the reservoir and the column of soil aggregates, variations in virus concentration in the [SOURCE: Page_216]

reservoir may be written: [SOURCE: Page_216]

�(�r +�r−c )       � =           × ((�m + �m−c ) − (�r + �r−c ))               (1) ��             �r [SOURCE: Page_216]

where t is the time (s), Q the water flow (ml s-1), Vr the volume of water in the reservoir (ml), [SOURCE: Page_216]

Cr and Cm the concentrations of free viruses (GC ml-1) in the reservoir and in the mobile water [SOURCE: Page_216]

between aggregates within the column, respectively, and Cr-c and Cm-c the concentrations of [SOURCE: Page_216]

viruses adsorbed on suspended colloids (GC ml-1) in the reservoir and in the mobile water, [SOURCE: Page_216]

respectively. [SOURCE: Page_216]

Virus concentration in the mobile water depends simultaneously on exchange with the [SOURCE: Page_216]

reservoir, on exchange of free viruses with intra-aggregate immobile water, and on possible [SOURCE: Page_216]

adsorption of free viruses at the aggregate outer surface. Considering that we could neglect [SOURCE: Page_216]

colloid immobilization on aggregate outer surface, we assumed that only free viruses can [SOURCE: Page_216]

diffuse within the aggregates: [SOURCE: Page_216]

Results and Discussion [SOURCE: Page_219]

15 [SOURCE: Page_219]

The mean of the decimal logarithm of the initial virus concentration in the reservoir (8.4) was [SOURCE: Page_220]

lower than the value calculated from the dilution of 10 µl of the initial virus suspension into [SOURCE: Page_220]

40 ml (9.5), and their standard deviation (0.5) was higher than the standard deviation resulting [SOURCE: Page_220]

from RT-qPCR uncertainty (< 0.1). These observations could result from possible virus [SOURCE: Page_220]

removals at the air-water-solid triple interface of 10 μl aliquots before freezing and after [SOURCE: Page_220]

thawing, since the triple interface length – to – volume ratio of such aliquots was high [SOURCE: Page_220]

(Thompson et al., 1998). [SOURCE: Page_220]

Impact of temperature, aggregate size, soil saturation procedure and water filtration on virus [SOURCE: Page_220]

concentrations [SOURCE: Page_220]

The means (and standard deviations) of the differences between decimal logarithms of virus [SOURCE: Page_220]

concentrations between 0.45 µm-filtered and unfiltered samples in immobilization [SOURCE: Page_220]

experiments were 0.10 (± 0.16), −0.34 (± 0.20) and 0.05 (± 0.25) log^10 GC ml-1 for soil [SOURCE: Page_220]

columns saturated with artificial soil solution 17 hours before contamination, soil columns [SOURCE: Page_220]

saturated with artificial soil solution 15 minutes before contamination, and soil columns [SOURCE: Page_220]

saturated with artificial soil solution enriched in fulvic acid, respectively. This suggested that [SOURCE: Page_220]

virus adsorbed on colloids dispersed in the moving solution could be neglected relative to free [SOURCE: Page_220]

viruses. By contrast, the addition of MgCl$_2$ led to a mean difference of 0.71 (± 0.90) log^10 GC [SOURCE: Page_220]

ml-1 between filtered and unfiltered soil solutions. [SOURCE: Page_220]

Experimental conditions gave negligible virus immobilization over 24 hours, and so did [SOURCE: Page_220]

not allow aggregate size effect be highlighted by comparing results for batch, 3–4 mm [SOURCE: Page_220]

aggregate column, and 4–5 mm aggregate column. In addition, the coefficient for virus [SOURCE: Page_220]

exchange e estimated by fitting simulations to experimental data for virus immobilization [SOURCE: Page_220]

over 1 week and virus subsequent remobilizations over 7 hours suggest that real aggregate [SOURCE: Page_220]

16 [SOURCE: Page_220]

sizes were about one tenth their initial sizes. This agrees with the poor soil structural stability [SOURCE: Page_221]

during its wetting, well known for phaeozems, which probably results from the covering of [SOURCE: Page_221]

clay particles by humic organic compounds (Bornand et al., 1984). [SOURCE: Page_221]

The impacts of temperature and saturation procedures (under vacuum conditions or under [SOURCE: Page_221]

atmospheric air pressure) were also assessed, but were found non-significant (data not [SOURCE: Page_221]

shown). [SOURCE: Page_221]

Reversible immobilization and irreversible removal in saturated conditions over a week [SOURCE: Page_221]

For batch and column experiments on virus immobilization, we observed no decrease in virus [SOURCE: Page_221]

concentrations over the first 24 hours when the mobile solution was artificial soil solution [SOURCE: Page_221]

without enrichment in MgCl$_2$ or fulvic acids (data not shown). The reductions in virus [SOURCE: Page_221]

concentrations were presumably too small compared with the uncertainty of molecular [SOURCE: Page_221]

quantifications and possible heterogeneities of virus concentrations in the solution. [SOURCE: Page_221]

By contrast, column experiments over 1 week led to a reduction of about 3 log^10 in virus [SOURCE: Page_221]

concentrations (Figure 2a). The viruses that could be remobilized later with artificial soil [SOURCE: Page_221]

solution represented a small fraction of the viruses that previously disappeared in the soil [SOURCE: Page_221]

column after sampling and removal of the reservoir (Figure 2b). The quantities of viruses [SOURCE: Page_221]

eluted with beef extract from soil columns after remobilizations were barely equal to the [SOURCE: Page_221]

quantities of reversibly immobilized viruses that should have been recovered beyond the [SOURCE: Page_221]

7 hours of remobilization (Figure S$_3$, Supporting Information). Non-remobilized viruses may [SOURCE: Page_221]

have been irreversibly adsorbed and/or destroyed. [SOURCE: Page_221]

Remobilizations for the samples first subjected to 1, 2, 3 and 7 days of immobilization [SOURCE: Page_221]

could be divided into two periods (Figure 2b): (i) the first half-hour during which virus [SOURCE: Page_221]

17 [SOURCE: Page_221]

concentrations at the outlet of the columns Co(t) (GC ml-1) dropped rapidly in a non- [SOURCE: Page_222]

exponential time course, and (ii) the period beyond that, during which virus concentrations [SOURCE: Page_222]

decreased slowly in an approximately exponential pattern: [SOURCE: Page_222]

�o (�) ≈ �1 × 10−�2 ×�          for     � ≥ 0.5 ℎ                          (15) [SOURCE: Page_222]

where c^1 and c^2 are constants specific to each remobilization experiment. c^1 and c^2 were [SOURCE: Page_222]

estimated from linear regressions between log^10(Co(t)) and t, and the quantities of viruses that [SOURCE: Page_222]

should have been released after 0.5 hour �̂r (� ≥ 0.5) were approximated by the equation: [SOURCE: Page_222]

1            � �̂r (� ≥ 0.5) =               ×        × �1 × 10−�2 ×0.5                      (16) ln(10)×�2       �s [SOURCE: Page_222]

�̂r (� ≥ 0.5) varied with the final Cr(t) before the beginning of remobilization (Figure 3) [SOURCE: Page_222]

according to the following equation: [SOURCE: Page_222]

1 �̂r (� ≥ 0.5) = 8520 × (�r (�))1.9                                     (17) [SOURCE: Page_222]

1   � Extending Equation (15) to the first half-hour (i.e. � ×                     × �1) would have meant 2   �s [SOURCE: Page_222]

multiplying the calculated quantities by 1.08 to 1.38 (results not shown). [SOURCE: Page_222]

The lack of proportionality between �̂r (� ≥ 0.5) and �r (� = 0) suggests that [SOURCE: Page_222]

immobilization could be described using Freundlich isotherm (Equation (9)), rather than using [SOURCE: Page_222]

models based on a partition distribution coefficient kD (Equation (7)) or kinetic [SOURCE: Page_222]

immobilization and remobilization according to Equations (4) and (5a). We described the [SOURCE: Page_222]

reversible immobilization of viruses using with nF = 1.53 and kF = 1120 to fit simultaneously [SOURCE: Page_222]

virus immobilization and remobilization experiments (Figure 2a, b). Differences between [SOURCE: Page_222]

Equation (17) and Freundlich isotherm with these last estimates of nF and kF probably result [SOURCE: Page_222]

partly from adsorbed viruses being released during the first half-hour in remobilization [SOURCE: Page_222]

experiments. For the estimated kF and nF values, the soil could retain approximately 103.70 GC [SOURCE: Page_222]

18 [SOURCE: Page_222]

g-1 soil, 105.66 GC g-1 soil and 108.93 GC g-1, for virus concentrations of 101 GC ml-1, [SOURCE: Page_223]

104 GC ml-1 and 109 GC ml-1, respectively. [SOURCE: Page_223]

Regarding the value of kir = 1.23 × 10-4 ml g-1 s-1, it appears that our soil was able to [SOURCE: Page_223]

definitively immobilize or destroy 90% of the viruses in solution in approximatively 56 hours [SOURCE: Page_223]

in our experimental conditions. We did not observe any impact of the "air-water-poly- [SOURCE: Page_223]

propylene" interface on murine norovirus concentration (results not shown) as observed by [SOURCE: Page_223]

Thompson et al. (1998) for MS$_2$ bacteriophage. [SOURCE: Page_223]

For our phaeozem containing nearly 60% clay and exposed to a concentration of [SOURCE: Page_223]

104 GC ml-1 of free viruses in solution, the estimated concentration of reversibly immobilized [SOURCE: Page_223]

viruses Sr (4.6 × 105 GC g-1) was median with regard to values estimated for various other real [SOURCE: Page_223]

soils containing 5–35% clay (Tesson & Renault, 2017); for these last soils, minimum and [SOURCE: Page_223]

maximum adsorbed concentrations were approximately between one hundredth and one [SOURCE: Page_223]

hundred times more than our estimate (Tesson & Renault, 2017). Although virus adsorption [SOURCE: Page_223]

may increase with the cation exchange capacity of soils (Lipson & Stotzky, 1983), other [SOURCE: Page_223]

factors affect it, including the nature and quantities of cations in solution, and the presence of [SOURCE: Page_223]

metal oxides (Cao et al., 2010; Syngouna & Chrysikopoulos, 2010). A 0.38 log^10 irreversible [SOURCE: Page_223]

daily removal of viruses was estimated for the phaeozem. It may have resulted from [SOURCE: Page_223]

irreversible immobilization, but the fact that no virus was recovered after an attempt to elute [SOURCE: Page_223]

them from the soil with beef extract suggests their destruction. This process has most often [SOURCE: Page_223]

been ignored in immobilization studies, suggesting that it may be neglected at time scales of [SOURCE: Page_223]

immobilization experiments. However, some data on irreversible removal rates have been [SOURCE: Page_223]

published: the immobilization rates then ranged between 0.001 and 864 day-1 (Tesson & [SOURCE: Page_223]

Renault, 2017). [SOURCE: Page_223]

Impact of soil drying on the reversibility of immobilizations [SOURCE: Page_223]

19 [SOURCE: Page_223]

After 24 hours of virus immobilization in column experiments saturated with contaminated [SOURCE: Page_224]

artificial soil solution, and partial drying to soil moisture 0.17 g g-1, 0.24 g g-1 and 0.36 g g-1 [SOURCE: Page_224]

dry weight basis following 1–2 days of desiccation, the columns were gently saturated again [SOURCE: Page_224]

with virus-free artificial soil solution to prevent the break-up of soil aggregates. [SOURCE: Page_224]

Remobilization experiments were then carried out with virus-free soil solution (Figure S$_4$, [SOURCE: Page_224]

Supporting Information). The observed concentration differences between remobilization [SOURCE: Page_224]

experiments on columns submitted to partial desiccation or not could also be observed on [SOURCE: Page_224]

virus initial concentrations in the reservoirs before contamination, so may be due to lower [SOURCE: Page_224]

virus concentration in some aliquots. The final state of viruses at the end of the desiccation [SOURCE: Page_224]

periods closely depends on the final soil moisture and aggregate bulk density. With an [SOURCE: Page_224]

aggregate bulk density b-a of about 2.0 g ml-1 for this soil, the aggregates were never [SOURCE: Page_224]

desaturated, even for the lowest experimental soil moisture. This could explain the lack of a [SOURCE: Page_224]

drying effect, as water suction probably remained lower than about 2 MPa, ensuring that all [SOURCE: Page_224]

the pores of diameter greater than 150 nm remained water-saturated (Fiès, 1992). [SOURCE: Page_224]

Impact of geochemistry on immobilization and subsequent desorption [SOURCE: Page_224]

Virus immobilization was assessed according to several different geochemical characteristics [SOURCE: Page_224]

of the circulating solution, with artificial soil solution alone or enriched by either 10 mM [SOURCE: Page_224]

MgCl$_2$ or 1.25 mg of fulvic acids. While no significant effect of adding fulvic acids was [SOURCE: Page_224]

observed, the addition of MgCl$_2$ led to an important immobilization of viral particles, with a [SOURCE: Page_224]

rapid decrease in about 3 hours of the concentrations of viruses in unfiltered samples of more [SOURCE: Page_224]

than 2 log^10 GC ml-1 and concentrations of viruses in filtered samples being approximately [SOURCE: Page_224]

one tenth of the concentrations in unfiltered samples, except at the last time (Figure 4). After [SOURCE: Page_224]

20 [SOURCE: Page_224]

the first 3 hours of immobilization, the virus concentrations increased and tended toward [SOURCE: Page_225]

values probably equal to about one-tenth of the initial concentration, probably with a [SOURCE: Page_225]

narrowing of the differences between filtered and unfiltered samples; unfortunately, we could [SOURCE: Page_225]

not determine whether the final concentrations of filtered and unfiltered samples were equal. [SOURCE: Page_225]

Viruses in filtered samples were considered free, while viruses immobilized on the filters [SOURCE: Page_225]

were considered adsorbed on dispersed colloids. As partial exchange of Mg$_2$+ in solution with [SOURCE: Page_225]

cations retained on soil cation exchange sites (mainly Ca$_2$+) led to 3.1 mM Mg$_2$+ final [SOURCE: Page_225]

concentration, we assumed an effect of [Mg$_2$+] on constants k^1, k^2 and kD' (Supporting [SOURCE: Page_225]

Information). In doing so, we obtained simulated values close to experimental ones during the [SOURCE: Page_225]

first 3 hours (Figure 4); beyond this time, the re-increase in free virus concentrations was [SOURCE: Page_225]

approximately simulated, while the re-increase in the concentration of viruses adsorbed on [SOURCE: Page_225]

dispersed colloids was greatly underestimated. This last difference probably results from [SOURCE: Page_225]

simultaneous variations in dispersed colloid concentrations that are not described in the [SOURCE: Page_225]

model. Moreover, kF value did not affect virus immobilization in those conditions, suggesting [SOURCE: Page_225]

that virus immobilization within soil aggregates could be neglected, compared to virus [SOURCE: Page_225]

immobilization on aggregate outer surface and dispersed colloids. [SOURCE: Page_225]

Remobilization experiments were also conducted on aggregate soil columns previously [SOURCE: Page_225]

exposed to 24 hours of contamination at 20°C and contaminated artificial soil solution. [SOURCE: Page_225]

Taking into account the likely differences between columns for remobilizations with on [SOURCE: Page_225]

artificial soil solution, and with either groundwater (−1.3 log^10 GC ml-1) or autoclaved [SOURCE: Page_225]

wastewater (−0.35 log^10 GC ml-1), we did not observe any large differences between the [SOURCE: Page_225]

column exposed to virus-free artificial soil solution and the one exposed to autoclaved [SOURCE: Page_225]

wastewater (Figure S$_4$, Supporting Information), probably because of the simultaneous higher [SOURCE: Page_225]

concentrations in soluble organic matter and mineral cations in wastewater, the effects of [SOURCE: Page_225]

which have counteracted each other. By contrast, remobilization of viruses for the [SOURCE: Page_225]

21 [SOURCE: Page_225]

contaminated column exposed to groundwater gave lower remobilization, probably resulting [SOURCE: Page_226]

from the groundwater higher Mg$_2$+ concentration (Table S$_1$, Supporting Information). [SOURCE: Page_226]

Conclusions and perspectives [SOURCE: Page_226]

Our aim was to collect new experimental data for a calcaric phaeozem and write a [SOURCE: Page_226]

mathematical model to describe reversible virus immobilization and irreversible removal (due [SOURCE: Page_226]

to irreversible immobilization and/or virus degradation). To our knowledge, our study is the [SOURCE: Page_226]

first one on a real soil containing more than 35% clay (Tesson & Renault, 2017). The clay [SOURCE: Page_226]

content and its nature, together with the organic matter content, give this phaeozem a high [SOURCE: Page_226]

cation exchange capacity; while this value is often positively correlated to virus [SOURCE: Page_226]

immobilization, our phaeozem retained few murine noroviruses, possibly owing to complex [SOURCE: Page_226]

association between smectite and organic matter, well known in Limagne Noire soils [SOURCE: Page_226]

(Bornand et al., 1984). In addition, the high daily virus removal in this phaeozem could have [SOURCE: Page_226]

resulted from the simultaneous presence of iron oxides and a solution pH just above neutrality [SOURCE: Page_226]

(Zhuang & Jin, 2008). [SOURCE: Page_226]

It was possible to simulate virus fate in this phaeozem with a model combining free and [SOURCE: Page_226]

colloidal transport of viruses in mobile water, exchange of free viruses between mobile and [SOURCE: Page_226]

immobile waters, kinetic virus removal, and reversible virus adsorptions on suspended [SOURCE: Page_226]

colloids and aggregate surfaces, and within aggregates. For experiments without added Mg$_2$+, [SOURCE: Page_226]

colloidal transport of viruses could be neglected owing to their low immobilization on soil. [SOURCE: Page_226]

By contrast, Mg$_2$+ enrichment greatly increased virus adsorption, and so colloidal transport of [SOURCE: Page_226]

viruses had to be taken into account, together with virus adsorption on aggregate outer [SOURCE: Page_226]

surfaces, outreaching the simple model of exchange between mobile and immobile water. [SOURCE: Page_226]

22 [SOURCE: Page_226]

Practically, a first approximation of the virus average residence time in this phaeozem is [SOURCE: Page_227]

probably more than 8 days, corresponding to 3 log^10 virus removal. However, viruses brought [SOURCE: Page_227]

to the soil quickly disperse in the water, especially in its mobile fraction, which may then be [SOURCE: Page_227]

easily absorbed by plant roots. [SOURCE: Page_227]

## [EMAIL_REDACTED]

I. Introduction [SOURCE: Page_248]

Human enteric viruses brought to the soil by contaminated irrigation water can reach and contaminate plant or groundwater while remaining fully infective for weeks, and then cause waterborne and foodborne diseases. However, virus immobilization or inactivation within the soil may delay or prevent their transfer. In order to better assess those processes and the resulting sanitary hazards, models have been published. In this paper, we collected data from about 27 papers dealing simultaneously with reversible immobilization of viruses within the soil or in (mixtures of) soil material(s) (clay, sand ...) and irreversible virus removal, without always being able to distinguish between virus destruction and irreversible immobilization. [SOURCE: Page_248]

II. Reversible virus immobilisation [SOURCE: Page_248]

Some models distinguish kinetic reversible immobilization of free viruses in soil solution, from their kinetic remobilization: [SOURCE: Page_248]

�� = ��−� × �� × ��                                       (1a) [SOURCE: Page_248]

�� = ��−� × �� × ��                                      (1b) [SOURCE: Page_248]

where Φa and Φd are the adsorption and desorption fluxes of viruses per volume of bulk soil, respectively (mol day-1 ml-1), b the bulk density of aggregates (g ml-1), Ci the concentration of free viruses in soil solution (virus ml-1), Sr the concentration of reversibly adsorbed viruses [SOURCE: Page_248]

2 [SOURCE: Page_248]

(virus g-1), ka-r the coefficient for reversible adsorption on soil solids (ml g-1 day-1), and kd-r the coefficient for desorption of reversibly adsorbed viruses (day-1). [SOURCE: Page_249]

For high ka-r and kd-r values and for a constant ka-r-to-kd-r ratio, Ci and Sr satisfy the following equilibrium: ��   � = ��−� = ��                                              (2) ��     �−� [SOURCE: Page_249]

where kD is the partition coefficient (ml g-1). The adsorbed virus concentration Sr is then proportional to the concentration of free viruses suspended in water Ci. Equation (2) may be expressed in decimal logarithms: [SOURCE: Page_249]

���(�� ) = ���(�� ) + ���(�� )                                      (3) [SOURCE: Page_249]

Because this assumption is not valid when adsorption sites differ from each other and/or adsorption cannot exceed a maximum, other mathematical formalisms have been proposed. [SOURCE: Page_249]

Freundlich isotherm corresponds to the following relationship between Ci and Sr: 1 �� = �� × �� ��                                             (4) [SOURCE: Page_249]

where nF is a parameter that indirectly describes the deviation from the proportional model involving kD partition coefficient, and kF a proportionality parameter. Equation (4) is often expressed in decimal logarithms: 1 ���(�� ) = ���(�� ) + � × ���(�� )                                    (5) � [SOURCE: Page_249]

As in the kD partition model, there is no maximum adsorption limit in the Freundlich isotherm model. [SOURCE: Page_249]

Langmuir isotherm corresponds to the following relationship between Ci and Sr: � �� = ����−� × (�+� � )                                            (6) � [SOURCE: Page_249]

3 [SOURCE: Page_249]

where Smax-r is the maximal concentration of reversibly adsorbed viruses (virus g-1), and β the concentration of free viruses in soil solution (virus ml-1) that leads to half of the reversible adsorption sites occupied. Equation (6) may be expressed in decimal logarithms: � ���(�� ) = ���(����−� ) + ��� (�+� � )                                (7) � [SOURCE: Page_250]

Main features of the models and the corresponding parameter values are reported in Tables 1 and 2. In Figure 1, adsorbed virus concentrations Sr are represented as a function of free virus concentrations Ci in soil solution. In Figure 2, the estimated Sr values for Ci=104 virus ml-1 are represented as a function of soil clay content. [SOURCE: Page_250]

44%, 28%, 36% and 4% of the publications mentioned in this paper employed the kinetic model, the kD partition model, Freundlich isotherm and/or Langmuir isotherm, respectively; three of the publications compared two of these models (Table 1). For a fixed concentration of free viruses in soil solution (e.g. 104 virus ml-1), deviation between minimum and maximum concentration of reversibly adsorbed viruses is about 6 log^10 (see Figures 1 and 2). Most Freundlich isotherms use a nF value very close to 1, making these isotherms de facto close to the kD partition model. Due to the various forces involved in virus adsorption, it is difficult to highlight obvious relationships between variables. [SOURCE: Page_250]

4 [SOURCE: Page_250]

## 1E+00 0   20         40         60           80       100 CLAY (%)

Figure 2: Relationships between reversibly adsorbed virus concentrations Sr for free virus concentrations Ci =104 virus ml-1, and soil clay contents (%) that were reported or estimated from the soil textural triangle. (Red circle accounts for iron oxide supplemented sands). [SOURCE: Page_258]

12 [SOURCE: Page_258]

III. Irreversible virus removal [SOURCE: Page_259]

Among the 27 papers dealing with reversible virus adsorption, irreversible virus removal could be neglected over periods not exceeding the duration of reversible virus adsorption experiments in 4 papers (Dowd et al., 1998; Cao et al., 2010; Syngouna & Chrysikopoulos, 2010; Sasidharan et al., 2017). In addition, results were not clear enough in five other papers (Burge & Enkiri, 1978; Chrysikopoulos & Syngouna, 2012; Chrysikopoulos & Aravantinou, 2014), and the estimated inactivation coefficients were probably biased due inappropriate partition coefficient kD for reversible adsorption in another paper (Yates & Ouyang, 1992). Lastly, inactivation resulted mainly from the triple phase (air-liquid-solid (tube wall)) boundary in a last paper (Thompson et al., 1998). Therefore, the following analysis focuses on the other 17 publications. [SOURCE: Page_259]

When irreversible virus removal was taken into account, it was described by first order kinetics. For irreversible removal from the soil solution, this may be written: ��� = − × ��                                       (8) �� [SOURCE: Page_259]

where  is a kinetic coefficient (day-1). This equation leads to:  −         � �� (�) = �� (� = 0) × � −� = �� (� = 0) × 10 ��(10)                   (9) [SOURCE: Page_259]

For irreversible removal from the viruses reversibly adsorbed on the soil, equation (8) could be transposed as: ��� �� = − × ��                                       (10) [SOURCE: Page_259]

When the quantity of reversibly adsorbed viruses can be described by the kD partition model, the irreversible removal of reversibly adsorbed viruses leads to simultaneous change of virus concentration in soil solution according to equation (8) with the same rate coefficient . [SOURCE: Page_259]

When the quantity of reversibly adsorbed viruses can be described by Freundlich isotherms, the irreversible removal of reversibly adsorbed viruses leads to the simultaneous change of virus concentration in soil solution according to the following equation: ��� �� = − × �� × ��                                    (11) [SOURCE: Page_259]

13 [SOURCE: Page_259]

The coefficient rates of the published first order rates models of virus irreversible removals are reported in Table 3, as well as the durations of the corresponding experiments and/or simulations. In addition, coefficient rate values are reported as a function of the iron oxides of the corresponding soil or soil material(s) in Figure 3. [SOURCE: Page_260]

85 non-zero inactivation rate coefficients were reported in Table 3. They varied between 0.001 and 864 day-1. No significant relationships were noted between coefficient rate values and other values that could affect it, e.g. with the soil clay content (Figure 3), probably because it depends on the simultaneous validation of different condition. [SOURCE: Page_260]

14 [SOURCE: Page_260]

Conc. virus (log^10 virus par g mat. fraiche) Irrigation    Irrigation     Irrigation                                                                        Irrigation contaminante enterrée     de surface     aérienne [SOURCE: Page_272]

Durée irrigation 1 min 2 min 30 s [SOURCE: Page_272]

Date (jour) Trois modes d'irrigation d'oignons verts (Allium) sous serre avec une irrigation quotidienne et à une date unique irrigation par une solution contaminée en virus [SOURCE: Page_272]

contamination de surface des bulbes. A partir d'expérimentations sur de petites colonnes d'agrégats du sol de Limagne Noire saturées en eau, nous avons montré que les virus apportés disparaissaient progressivement de la solution circulant dans la colonne : leur concentration était divisée par 2,4 environ toutes les 24 heures. L'utilisation d'une solution non contaminée pour "rincer" ce sol ne permettait de récupérer qu'une très faible fraction des virus disparus et l'utilisation d'un protocole "plus violent" défini pour maximiser la remobilisation des virus restant adsorbés à la surface des particules de sol ne permettait de récupérer que très peu de virus en plus, suggérant la destruction de ces virus. La composition chimique de la solution traversant le sol impactait la disparition des virus ou leur remobilisation. Notamment de fortes teneurs en ion magnésium favorisaient l'immobilisation du virus ou ralentissaient sa remobilisation, comme cela a été observé avec de l'eau                                                              1.E+10 Abattement (7 jours) Concentration virus (virus par ml) [SOURCE: Page_272]

de nappe prélevée en Limagne Noire. = 3 log^10 Malgré l'abattement provoqué par le sol sur la quantité                                                         1.E+09 de virus pouvant migrer vers les racines, on a observé une internalisation de virus dans les oignons vers avec                                                             1.E+08 contamination des bulbes et des tiges-feuilles. Contrairement aux contaminations de surface des tiges-                                                              1.E+07 feuilles dont la contamination dépend du mode d'irrigation, les contaminations internalisées des bulbes et                                                        1.E+06 des tiges feuilles ne varient pas avec le mode d'irrigation,                                                                            Mesuré probablement parce que les racines voient à peu près les                                                            1.E+05              Modèle mêmes quantités d'eau avec les mêmes niveaux de contaminations.                                                                                                     1.E+04 Ces travaux doivent être poursuivis pour tester la                                                                       0     24    48   72    96 120 144 168 192 possibilité d'une décontamination passive associée à la Temps de séjour dans le sol (heures) transpiration ou à la guttation de la plante, évaluer l'impact de molécules comme les huiles essentielles sur              Photos du dispositif utilisé pour suivre le devenir du l'inactivation des virus, et voire au-delà de la culture          norovirus murin (c.à.d. de souris) dans des colonnes de sol l'impact des opérations post-récoltes sur le risque.                 et évolution sur 7 jours de leur concentration dans la solution circulante. Rejet ou réutilisation ? Les risques viraux sensu stricto inhérents aux deux scénarios très contrastés que sont les rejets d'eaux usées traitées en rivière et leur réutilisation en irrigation agricole restent à évaluer en prenant en compte leurs impacts respectifs sur les risques de contamination de l'Homme par les eaux, les aliments ou l'air. Cette évaluation peut nécessiter de subdiviser la population en sous-groupes se distinguant par leurs habitudes et leur sensibilité a priori aux pathogènes. Elle peut nécessiter de prendre en compte explicitement une variabilité des phénomènes, surtout pour les populations de petites tailles, avec l'utilisation de techniques de Monte Carlo pour générer une variabilité de symptômes et d'excrétions virales entre individus souffrant tous de gastroentérites d'étiologie virale. [SOURCE: Page_273]
